the Band’s water quality requirements for reservation waters.”46 The NorthMet project site is 
located over 116 river miles upstream from the Fond du Lac Reservation.  
While WUWC members may not know exactly how many of their facilities are likely to be 
impacted by the proposed rule, they believe that EPA’s analysis understates the economic 
impacts of its action. When a proposed rule has the potential to have far-reaching economic 
impacts, as this rule does, it is critical that the agency’s authority be very clear.47 For the reasons 
explained above, WUWC members do not believe that to be the case.  
2. The baseline tribal WQS are likely to create jurisdictional confusion and 
intergovernmental disputes. 
WUWC members are concerned that the proposed rule will substantially increase compliance 
complexity and intergovernmental disputes. The states in which WUWC members are located 
have spent decades establishing science-based water quality standards that recognize each state’s 
unique topography, hydrology, geology, and climate. State WQS protect designated uses and are 
“based on sound scientific rationale.”48 The states have considered “the water quality standards 
of downstream waters and [ensured] that its water quality standards provide for the attainment 
and maintenance of the water quality standards of downstream waters,” as the CWA required.49 
And they have employed EPA’s section 304(a) recommendations, modified to reflect site-
specific conditions, or other scientifically defensible methods to establish WQS.50  
Every three years, states must review their quality criteria, water uses and requirements for 
antidegradation.51 These proceedings are time-consuming, involved, and iterative. In California, 
for example, reviews are handled by basin and involve evaluating new scientific and technical 
information; addressing new legal requirements; responding to EPA’s recommendations and 
guidelines; ensuring compliance with State Water Board policy requirements; and addressing 
stakeholder concerns. Arizona has adopted a “local control approach” to ensure protection of the 
state’s important water resources through a public hearing process. When it determines that a 
revision of a surface WQS is needed, Arizona conducts a rulemaking for the proposed revisions. 
Each state follows its own process, but all carefully consider a variety of factors, including the 
 
46 See Clean Water Act Section 401(a)(2) Evaluation and Recommendations with respect to the Fond du 
Lac Band’s Objection to the Proposed Clean Water Act Section 404 Permit for the NorthMet Mine 
Project, U.S. EPA Region 5 (Apr. 29, 2022), https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2022-
05/EPA%20CWA%20401a2%20Evaluation%20and%20Recommendations%20to%20the%20Corps%20
on%20NorthMet%204-29-22.pdf. 
47 West Virginia v. EPA, 142 S. Ct. 2587, 2609 (2022) (citing Utility Air Regul. Grp. v. EPA, 573 U.S. 
302, 324 (2014). 
48 40 C.F.R. §131.11(a). 
49 40 C.F.R. §131.10(b). 
50 See 40 C.F.R. §§ 131.11(b), 131.6, 131.5(a). If EPA determines that the standards are consistent with 
federal requirements, it must approve the standards within 60 days of the date of submission. 33 U.S.C. 
§ 1313(c)(3); 40 C.F.R. § 131.5(b). If the state fails to adopt the changes within 90 days of notification, 
EPA must promulgate a water quality standard for the state. Id.  
51 33 U.S.C. § 1313(c)(3)–(4). 

 
 
10 
aquatic communities, hydrology, geologic formations, and anthropogenic influences affecting 
intrastate waters, and all conduct research into new technologies, weigh alternatives, and involve 
the public.  
EPA cannot account for local factors in promulgating federal baseline WQS for Indian country 
and offer the robust public proceedings needed to ensure that the WQS established are 
appropriate, considering section 303(c)’s enumerated uses. EPA maintains that “as both the 
promulgating entity and the primary implementing authority,” it will allow “a degree of site-
specific tailoring” that will “be accomplished by use of the narrative criteria and binding 
translation procedures identified in the rule.”52 That only underscores why establishing baseline 
WQS for all of Indian country is inappropriate. EPA’s proposed use of narrative criteria, 
translated into numeric criteria using one of five options at EPA’s discretion, will likely make it 
very difficult for regulated entities to plan.  
The legal and jurisdictional complexity throughout Indian country is, in fact, one of WUWC’s 
major concerns. EPA intends for the proposed rule to apply throughout “Indian county,” except 
with respect to: (1) reservations where tribes have been granted TAS status; (2) reservations 
where EPA has “explicitly found that a state has jurisdiction to adopt water quality standards”; 
(3) off-reservation allotments and off-reservation dependent Indian communities; and (4) 
reservations where the Regional Administrator approves an exclusion.53 WUWC believes that 
EPA’s proposed scope is overbroad and will lead to uncertainty and conflict. 
First, Indian land tenure is variable and often highly fractured. Due to federal allotment policies, 
many reservations are a hodgepodge of tribal trust land, allotted lands, and fee lands. It is well-
established that states have jurisdiction over fee lands located within Indian country.54 As such, 
state WQS standards already apply to all intrastate waters on fee lands. The proposed rule cannot 
consequently apply to fee lands located within an Indian reservation because there is no 
“necessity” under section 303(c)(4)(B)(2). EPA does not have authority to supplant state WQS 
with its own baseline standards.   
The legal authorities governing reservations differ from reservation to reservation. In Colorado, 
for example, the Southern Ute Reservation has a checkerboard ownership pattern with many 
non-Indians living within the reservation’s historic boundaries, which has precipitated long and 
contentious jurisdictional disputes. Congress sought to resolve those disputes in 1984 with Public 
Law No. 98-290 (PL 290), which draws reservation boundaries and delineates the respective 
jurisdictional authority of the tribe, the state and the county. Despite PL 290, the state and the 
tribe do not agree as to territorial and regulatory jurisdiction concerning the administration of 
Clean Air Act programs relative to non-Indian air pollution sources on fee land within the 
reservation. Congress also passed tribe-specific allotment acts that can affect the allocation of 
jurisdiction, as well as various termination and restoration acts. In order to determine which 
 
52 88 Fed. Reg. at 29499. 
53 Id. at 29522 (§ 131.XX(a)). 
54 Nevada v. Hicks, 533 U. S. 353, 361 (2001) (“State sovereignty does not end at a reservation’s 
border.”); County of Yakima v. Confederated Tribes and Bands of Yakima Nation, 502 U. S. 251, 257–
258 (1992). 

 
 
11 
WQS applies, states and tribes will have to resolve jurisdictional uncertainties each of these 
unique authorities, which will likely generate intergovernmental disputes that would otherwise 
have been avoided.  
The number of very small reservations and off-reservation trust parcels will also create 
administrative headaches.55 Many tribes have acquired trust lands outside of reservation 
boundaries for a variety of reasons—including to support economic development important for 
tribal self-determination. But non-contiguous trust lands—of which there are many—only 
increase the jurisdictional complexity and make planning difficult for regulated entities. These 
problems will only increase. The Bureau of Indian Affairs (BIA) recently announced that it has 
an affirmative policy favoring trust acquisition.56 It “has processed thousands of applications 
placing over a million acres of land into trust for tribes and individual Indians since the passage 
of the IRA in 1934.”57 BIA has proposed changes to its fee-to-trust regulations that would allow 
it to “process applications more quickly and with less expense to applicants.”58 Among the 
proposed changes is a presumption that the Secretary of the Interior will acquire land in trust and 
the elimination from BIA’s consideration of “jurisdictional problems or conflicts of land use 
which may arise.”59 The combination of EPA’s and BIA’s proposed rules are likely to have far-
reaching consequences to water pollution management.  
For WUWC members, having a checkerboard application of variable WQS can make its 
compliance efforts very challenging. Administration complexity will be exacerbated by EPA’s 
proposed use of narrative criteria, which EPA will numerically translate using one of five 
options. With over 100 recognized tribes in California, 29 in Washington, 23 in New Mexico, 22 
in Arizona, 20 in Nevada, eight in Utah, and two in Colorado, our members’ concerns are well-
founded. In WUWC’s view, the far more sensible approach—and the one most consistent with 
current practice and the plain language of the Act—is to apply state WQS until such time as a 
tribe—in an exercise of its sovereign judgment—decides to seek TAS status under section 518.  
Specific Comments 
1. § 131XX(a) Scope. 
The proposed rule will apply to “all waters of the United States in Indian country,” except for: 
reservations where EPA has already promulgated WQS (i.e., the Confederated Tribes of the 
Colville Reservation); states where EPA has “explicitly found that a state has jurisdiction” or 
where a tribe is authorized TAS; off-reservation allotments and dependent Indian communities; 
and reservations were a Regional Administrator approves an exclusion.60 Exclusions are 
 
55 EPA intends for the proposed rule to apply all trust lands, not just formal reservations. 88 Fed. Reg. at 
29522 (§ 131.XX(a)).  
56 Land Acquisitions, 87 Fed. Reg. 74334, 74335 (Dec. 5, 2022). 
57 Id. (“[I]t will now be clear Departmental policy to support land into trust.”). 
58 Id. 
59 See id. at 74337, 74338. 
60 88 Fed. Reg. at 29522. 

 
 
12 
permitted for tribes “that have a plan in place for establishing WQS for EPA approval or are 
working on a plan and do not yet have EPA-approved WQS for EPA in effect.”61  
For the reasons explained above, WUWC recommends that EPA limit the scope of the rule. First, 
EPA should clarify that the rule does not apply to fee lands located within Indian country. 
Second, EPA should consider limiting application of the rule to the definition of “Indian 
reservation” Congress enacted in section 518—“all land within the limits of any Indian 
reservation under the jurisdiction of the United States Government . . .”62  
WUWC also suggests that EPA not apply the rule to informal reservations to reduce the potential 
for conflicts and the cost of compliance. In addition, EPA should consider applying the proposed 
rule in phases, based on reservation size. The surface waters on small reservations and trust lands 
are far more likely to adequately protected by existing state WQS. To the extent that EPA 
believes that there are serious gaps that it needs to address in Indian country, it should focus its 
efforts on those locations. Doing so would also enable it to more accurately understand how the 
rule is affecting upstream states and dischargers.    
Finally, WUWC recommends that, rather than requiring tribes to seek an exclusion from the 
application of the rule, the rule offer an “opt in” process. Doing so would enable EPA to 
determine whether the proposed rule reflects and protects tribal priorities, rather than EPA’s, by 
empowering tribes to opt into its application. Many tribes may not have invested significant 
resources into evaluating EPA’s proposed rule for various reasons. Some may believe that state 
WQS adequately protect reservation waters. Others may wish to avoid the jurisdictional disputes 
the rule is likely to generate. EPA received comment letters from only ten tribes during tribal 
consultation. The low level of response, coupled with the concerns tribes voiced regarding 
jurisdictional disputes, the need for regionally-specific standards, and the application in 
checkerboard reservations, suggests that an opt-in approach would better reflect tribal priorities 
and respect tribal sovereignty.  
2. § 131.XX(b) Consultation with Tribes. 
Proposed § 131.XX(b) provides that in taking actions under this section, the Regional 
Administrator will initiate Tribal consultation with the tribe(s) whose interests may be affected, 
consistent with applicable EPA Tribal consultation policies. 
WUWC supports EPA’s consultation efforts and believes that such consultation is critical to 
understanding the local complexities that must be considered in establishing appropriate WQS. 
But EPA’s consultation should not be limited to tribes. The baseline WQS will directly impact 
the states in which the trust lands are located, most dramatically where Indian land tenure is 
complex and fractured. Because the proposed rule purports to regulate all waters within Indian 
country (subject to certain exceptions), EPA will assume regulatory control over entities 
operating under state permits that apply state WQS. In addition, the upstream state and 
discharges will also be directly impacted. Consulting only with tribes regarding EPA’s WQS will 
 
61 Id. at 29501. 
62 33 U.S.C. § 1377(g)(1). 

 
 
13 
result in EPA not having sufficient information in translating its narrative criteria into numeric 
standards.  
In addition, many tribes do not have staff or extensive experience in water pollution 
management. That may be because of lack of resources or tribes may have elected to rely on 
state WQS, focusing their efforts on other tribal priorities. In any case, states have entire 
agencies devoted to water pollution management and over a half-century of experience in 
implementing section 303. EPA, which has little local knowledge, should involve the states in 
establishing appropriate WQS. WUWC also recommends that EPA consult with non-tribal 
entities that will be directly affected by the proposed rule.  
3. § 131.XX(c) Federal Baseline Designated Uses 
EPA proposes to establish three baseline designated uses for most of Indian country: (1) the 
protection and propagation of aquatic life, which includes protection of human health of 
consumers of aquatic life; (2) primary contact recreation; and (3) cultural and traditional uses of 
reservation waters.63 Respectfully, WUWC members are concerned that EPA has not and cannot 
meet basic section 303 requirements in implementing the rule. 
Pursuant to the regulations EPA promulgated to implement its WQS program, states and 
authorized tribes are required to specify “appropriate water uses to be achieved and protected.”64 
In doing so, they must “take into consideration the water quality standards of downstream waters 
and [to] ensure that its water quality standards provide for the attainment and maintenance of the 
water quality standards of downstream waters.”65 The proposed rule does not and cannot do 
either of those things, which is likely to lead to significant implementation problems in the states 
WUWC members operate. The western United States is arid; there are many waters that do not 
support fish or primary contact recreation due to insufficient flows or other naturally occurring 
features. Attainment of EPA’s surface water quality criteria is not possible in all waters. It will 
be necessary to use site-specific criteria and/or attainability analyses—which require 
considerable technical expertise to develop—to prevent overly stringent effluent limits, 
unnecessary 303(d) listings, and unattainable TMDL endpoints.  
As EPA admits, its blanket approach to designated uses “may not be attainable in all Indian 
reservation waters because of Tribe-specific or site-specific factors.”66 To address this problem, 
EPA proposes a regulatory process to revise designated uses as “data and information may 
become available after the baseline WQS rule becomes final.”67 But this just underscores the 
problems with EPA’s “one size fits all” approach to WQS. WQS need to be tailed to site-specific 
conditions because aquatic communities, hydrology, geologic formations, and anthropogenic 
influences substantially vary. These resources should be evaluated by individual reservations, 
 
63 88 Fed. Reg. at 29522 (131.XX(c)). 
64 40 C.F.R. § 131.10(a). 
65 Id. § 131.10(b). 
66 88 Fed. Reg. at 29512. 
67 Id. at 29512; 29526 (131.XX(i)). 

 
 
14 
individual waters, and available resources for implementation before designated uses are 
identified.  
The proposed regulation provides that the Regional Administrator “may”—upon request of the 
tribe or upon the Regional Administrator’s identification—revise one or more designated uses to 
“more appropriately reflect the Tribe-specific use and value of waters.”68 But this approach turns 
the process on its head. Rather than designating appropriate uses at the outset, EPA’s blanket 
designations will have to be revised on a case-by-case basis, and the interim, regulated parties 
will face unattainable requirements. Moreover, because the rule does not allow states or 
regulated parties to request revision, the rule could leave states and regulated entities without 
recourse. Tribes that do not have expertise or are not otherwise concerned about off-reservation 
activities may be unmotivated to revise uses that were inappropriate from the start. States and 
upstream dischargers, however, will be directly and indirectly impacted by EPA’s designated 
uses.  
With respect to EPA’s proposed designation of cultural and traditional uses, it does not appear 
that EPA has authority to designate this use. Under section 303(c)(4), the Administrator can 
establish WQS only if he “determines that a revised or new standard is necessary to meet the 
requirements of this chapter.”69 As EPA’s regulations establish, the minimum requirements of 
the chapter are the use designations consistent with sections 101(a)(2) and 303(c)(2).70 Section 
101(a)(2) uses include “the protection and propagation of fish, shellfish, and wildlife” and 
“recreation in and on the water.”71 Section 303(c)(2) provides that WQS are to be established 
“taking into consideration their use and value for public water supplies, propagation of fish and 
wildlife, recreational purposes, and agricultural, industrial, and other purposes, and also taking 
into consideration their use and value for navigation.”72 EPA reiterates in 40 C.F.R. § 131.10 that 
“[t]he classification of the waters of the State must take into consideration the use and value of 
water for public water supplies, protection and propagation of fish, shellfish and wildlife, 
recreation in and on the water, agricultural, industrial, and other purposes including navigation.” 
While states and authorized tribes can adopt non-101(a)(2) uses, but they must justify those uses 
before EPA will approve them.73   
EPA maintains that cultural and traditional uses serve “to protect the health and welfare of Tribal 
members exercising such uses and are thus within the purposes enumerated in the Act.”74 There 
is nothing in the CWA to support EPA’s interpretation, as its own regulations establish. In 40 
 
68 Id. at 29526 (131.XX(i)). 
69 33 U.S.C. § 1313(c)(4)(B) (emphasis added). 
70 40 C.F.R. § 131.6(a). 
71 33 U.S.C. § 1251(a)(2). 
72 Id. § 1313(c)(2)(A). 
73 40 C.F.R. § 131.10(a) (“If adopting new or revised designated uses other than the uses specified in 
section 101(a)(2) of the Act, or removing designated uses, States must submit documentation justifying 
how their consideration of the use and value of water for those uses listed in this paragraph appropriately 
supports the State's action.”). 
74 88 Fed. Reg. at 29503. 

 
 
15 
C.F.R. § 131.2, EPA interprets “‘Serve the purposes of the Act’ (as defined in sections 101(a)(2) 
and 303(c) of the Act)” to require WQS that “wherever attainable, provide water quality for the 
protection and propagation of fish, shellfish and wildlife and for recreation in and on the water 
and take into consideration their use and value of public water supplies, propagation of fish, 
shellfish, and wildlife, recreation in and on the water, and agricultural, industrial, and other 
purposes including navigation.” Cultural and traditional uses are not part of the Act’s purposes 
and, thus, are not “necessary to meet the requirements of this chapter” under section 303(c)(4).     
WUWC is not taking the position that tribes cannot independently designate cultural and 
traditional uses in establishing their own WQS.75 But it does not believe that EPA has the 
authority to do so under section 303(c)(4).76 
4. § 131.XX(d) Federal Baseline Water Quality Criteria 
EPA proposes to use narrative criteria only. Under the proposed rule, EPA can chose among five 
options to numerically translate the narrative criteria: 
1. EPA’s national recommended water quality criteria published under section 
304; 
2. EPA’s national recommended water quality criteria published under section 
304, modified to reflect site-specific conditions and aquatic communities that 
incorporate (where relevant): 
a. Fish consumption rates protective of tribal fish consumers; 
b. Site-specific water chemistry; 
c. Protective default chemistry inputs; or 
d. Other scientifically defensible assessments, such as endangered species 
guidance and indigenous knowledge; 
3. WQS adopted by the tribe or CWA-effective WQS applicable in “adjacent or 
other relevant state(s) or Tribe(s),” taking into account indigenous knowledge; 
4. The Water Quality Guidance for the Great Lakes System; or 
5. Existing CWA provisions in 40 C.F.R. part 131. 
WUWC members are concerned that this approach provides dischargers, permit writers, and 
other regulators with virtually no information regarding the standards they will have to satisfy. 
 
75 Id. 
76 WUWC is also concerned that EPA is “not proposing to define cultural and traditional uses in more 
detail in this rule because they can include a variety of uses specific to the ceremonies and traditions of 
each Tribe, and each use may require different levels of protection.” 88 Fed. Reg at 29503. Without 
identifying the uses or the levels of protection required or where these protections may apply, WUWC 
members cannot evaluate the potential impacts of the proposed rule on their facilities. 

 
 
16 
For regulated entities like WUWC members, the use of narrative criteria makes planning and 
compliance more difficult, while expanding EPA’s discretion.77   
Under option 2, EPA can modify its national recommended water quality criteria published 
under section 304, by adopting fish consumption rates largely as it sees fit. Thus, EPA can use its 
general fish consumption rate of 22 g/day or its national default subsistence value of 142 g/day, 
the latter of which would require most states to substantially reduce their WQS for downstream 
compliance purposes. Under this approach, Regional Administrators will have wide discretion to 
select the numeric criteria they prefer, without “taking into consideration their use and value for 
public water supplies, propagation of fish and wildlife, recreational purposes, and agricultural, 
industrial, and other purposes, and also taking into consideration their use and value for 
navigation,” as section 303(c) requires.78 And it appears that stricter standards are EPA’s goal, as 
the preamble explains that “a fish consumption rate should be selected that reflects consumption 
that is not suppressed by fish availability or concerns about the safety of fish for human 
consumption.”79 “[A] suppression effect is when the fish consumption rate for a given Tribe 
reflects a current level of consumption that is artificially diminished relative to the Tribe’s 
heritage fish consumption rate.”80 A tribe’s current level of fish consumption may differ from its 
“heritage fish consumption rate” for a wide variety of reasons, not the least of which is the wide-
spread availability of non-local food sources through supermarkets and other sources.   
It is also concerning that EPA’s justification for this option goes well beyond the purposes of the 
CWA. EPA explains that “[t]he negative impacts of suppression extend well beyond Tribal 
health, leading to consequences for Tribal economies and cultures as well.”81 “Given that aquatic 
resources often support a Tribe’s cultural self-determination and can be pivotal to the economic 
wellbeing of the community, impacts to these resources can affect the very foundation of Tribal 
social and political organization.” The CWA, however, was not designed to address effects 
extending “well beyond Tribal health.” As Congress expressly provided, the purpose of the 
CWA “is to restore and maintain the chemical, physical, and biological integrity of the Nation’s 
waters.”82 While WUWC members strongly support WQS that are protective of fish consumers, 
nothing in the CWA authorizes or provides legal support for EPA to require the use of 
“unsuppressed” fish consumption rates.83

(reviewing EPA’s translation of Massachusetts’ narrative criteria to significant limit phosphorus 
discharges under a “zone of reasonableness” standard); Am. Paper Inst. v. EPA, 996 F.2d 346, 350 (D.C. 
Cir. 1993) (deferring to EPA’s translation of narrative criteria).

82 33 U.S.C. § 1251(a). 
83 EPA takes the position that tribal treaty rights require the application of unsuppressed fish consumption 
rates and has proposed a rule that would require states to protect treaty rights in state WQS. See, e.g., 
Water Quality Standards Regulatory Revisions to Protect Tribal Reserved Rights, 88 Fed. Reg. 74361 

 
 
17 
WUWC members are unsure regarding how Regional Administrators will use indigenous 
knowledge to modify national recommended water quality criteria. EPA explains that Regional 
Administrators may “modify the CWA section 304(a) recommended criteria to protect site-
specific conditions based on a sound scientific rationale, including EPA published 
methodologies, if available, and, as appropriate, Indigenous Knowledge (IK), often referred to as 
Traditional Ecological Knowledge (TEK), where consistent with EPA’s regulations and CWA 
statutory requirements.”84 WUWC members have deep respect for indigenous communities and 
the traditional ecological knowledge such communities acquired over millennia. But EPA 
developed its national recommended water quality criteria “to accurately reflect the latest 
scientific knowledge on the impacts of pollutants on human health and the environment.”85 Its 
water quality criteria “are based solely on data and scientific judgments on the relationship 
between pollutant concentrations and environmental and human health effects.”86 It is not 
apparent how EPA would use IK to modify its data-based, scientific judgments on the 
relationship between pollutant concentrations and environmental and human health effects or 
why it would be prudent to do so. 
In any event, the Regional Administrator will translate the baseline criteria after the proposed 
rule is in effect using one of the five options, at its discretion.87 It “could,” but is not required to, 
provide a specific public process on EPA’s translation in advance of the public process 
associated with the implementation program.88 And if conflicts arise between upstream state 
WQS and baseline WQS, EPA will apply 40 C.F.R. § 131.7. Under that section, the Regional 
Administrator—who created the conflict in the first place—is responsible for attempting to 
resolve the dispute they created. The Regional Administrator also has the power to appoint a 
mediator or arbitrator, as applicable, and if the state refuses mediation or arbitration, the 
Regional Administrator may appoint an official to prepare a written recommendation to resolve 
the dispute. Whether an NPDES permittee, citizen, citizen group, or other affected entity may 
participate is also left to the discretion of the Regional Administrator. 
The Regional Administrator cannot possibly serve as a neutral arbiter in this situation. This 
approach is inconsistent with the CWA’s policy of ensuring that states (an authorized tribes) 
have the primary responsibility for preventing, reducing, and eliminating pollution.  
 
(Dec. 5, 2022). Under that rule states and authorized tribes would have to: (a) designate uses of 
waterbodies that expressly incorporate protections for tribal reserved rights; (b) establish water quality 
criteria to protect those rights; and (c) adopt antidegradation requirements to protect water quality to the 
level deemed necessary to protect tribal reserved rights. EPA has not identified any treaty language to 
support its position.  
84 EPA refers to indigenous knowledge as traditional ecological knowledge. 88 Fed. Reg. at 29507. 
85 U.S. EPA, Water Quality Criteria, https://www.epa.gov/wqc (last visited August 2, 2023). 
86 See e.g., U.S. EPA, Nat’l Recommend Water Quality Criteria 2002 1 (2002), 
https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/2018-12/documents/national-recommended-hh-criteria-2002.pdf. 
87 88 Fed. Reg. at 29508. 
88 Id.  

 
 
18 
5. 131.XX(e) Federal baseline antidegradation policy. 
WUWC members support antidegradation goals. However, WUWC members are concerned that 
EPA’s broad discretion in translating its water quality criteria into numeric standards discussed 
above will create significant compliance issues. While the Regional Administrator has the power 
to allow lowering of high water quality, the proposed rule requires written agreement from the 
tribe that “is necessary to accommodate important economic or social development in the area in 
which the waters are located.”89 This approach is likely to result in conflicts. If EPA imposes 
standards stricter than state WQS that dischargers cannot meet, tribes will be in a position to 
withhold the written agreement that the regulations require for a lowering of highwater quality. 
In fact, it may be necessary to obtain multiple permissions, depending on waterbody and the 
number of reservations and trust lands involved. 
6. 131.XX(f)(4)(i) Outstanding national resource water protection consistent with 
paragraph (e)(3) of this section. 
This subsection would allow the Regional Administrator to determine, with written agreement 
from the tribe, whether a reservation water qualifies as an Outstanding National Resource Water 
(ONRW). An ONRW, under the proposed rule, could include “such as waters of national and 
Tribal parks and wildlife refuges and waters of exceptional recreational, ecological, or cultural 
significance.”90 The proposed language differs from the current language found in 40 C.F.R. 
§ 131.12(a)(3), which does not include “cultural significance.”91 The proposed rule does not 
require notice to affected upstream states or seek their input prior to the Regional Administrator 
making the determination.  
EPA interprets 40 C.F.R. § 131.12(a)(3) “to mean no new or increased discharges to ONRWs 
and no new or increased discharge to tributaries to ONRWs that would result in lower water 
quality in the ONRWs.”92 In other words, designating waters as an ONRW imposes a nearly 
absolute ban on new or expanded point source discharges. Only “short-term, temporary water 
quality degradation” can be permitted, and “only if the short-term, temporary degradation is 
limited to the shortest possible time, does not impact existing uses, and does not alter the 
essential or special characteristics” that make it an ONRW.93 EPA interprets “short-term” to 
mean “weeks and months, not years.”94 
Given the very significant impact ONRW designation can have, WUWC members are concerned 
that the proposed rule allows the Regional Administrator too much discretion. The affected state 
and the public should be notified prior to the Regional Administrator making a determination, 
 
89 Id. at 29523. 
90 88 Fed. Reg. at 29523 (§131.XX(e)(3)). 
91 Id. 
92 U.S. EPA, Water Quality Standards Handbook, Ch. 4, 12 (2012), 
https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/2014-10/documents/handbook-chapter4.pdf.

and there should be meaningful standards for making such a determination. In addition, EPA 
should take into account the extent of the waters within and outside of Indian country and the 
economic effects of such designation. While WUWC members support anti-degradation rules, 
there should be clear standards and a more detailed process.  
Conclusion 
This rulemaking is an opportunity for EPA to address our comments and those of others who 
support CWA protections and ensuring that all intrastate waters nationwide are protected. Our 
members are experienced, on-the-ground partners with EPA and states in the implementation of 
the CWA. Based on this experience, WUWC is prepared to assist EPA other federal and state 
regulatory agencies, and members of Congress in addressing this important issue. WUWC looks 
forward to continued dialogue and collaboration as EPA completes its rulemaking.

Dear Administrator Fox: 
 
On May 5, 2023, the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) published a 
proposed rule titled, “Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian 
Reservations.” 88 FR at 29,496 (May 5, 2023).  The proposed rule is intended to 
establish nationwide water quality standards for Indian lands claiming that the 
majority of reservations currently lack Clean Water Act (CWA) protections.  
 
These comments are submitted the J.R. Simplot Company (Simplot).  Simplot is a 
privately held, agribusiness corporation based in Boise, Idaho.  Simplot is engaged 
in a number of businesses including vegetable and potato processing, farming, 
mining of phosphate and silica, fertilizer manufacturing, fertilizer distribution, 
ranching, beef cattle feedlots, beef processing, and other enterprises related to 
agriculture.  A number of these operations are subject to requirements of the Clean 
Water Act.  The proposed rule, if implemented as drafted, will have broad and 
substantial changes on Clean Water Act requirements for regulated entities 
upstream and near Indian lands.  Thus, this proposed rule is of direct interest to 
Simplot. 
 
As thoroughly discussed in the attached comment document, EPA has proposed 
a rule that is exceeds its statutory authority and is not in accordance with the law.  
The proposed rule is based on a flawed premise and would create a framework for 
establishing water quality criteria without any of the procedural requirements of the 
CWA, EPA’s regulations, and the Administrative Procedure Act (APA). Finally, the 
methodology to derive numeric criteria, especially for fish consumption rates is 
technically flawed and does not reflect the latest scientific knowledge. We urge 
EPA to withdraw the proposed rule.

Executive Summary  
EPA proposes to establish nationwide water quality standards for Indian lands claiming 
that the majority of reservations currently lack Clean Water Act (CWA) protections.  
However, as shown in these comments, a review of “treatment as states” (TAS) tribes 
shows that this claim is incorrect. Furthermore, the approach that EPA has proposed to 
“remedy” this “problem” is legally flawed. The proposed rule would also create a 
framework for establishing water quality criteria without any of the procedural 
requirements of the CWA, EPA’s regulations, and the Administrative Procedure Act 
(APA). Finally, the methodology to derive numeric criteria, especially for fish consumption 
rates is technically flawed and does not reflect the latest scientific knowledge. We urge 
EPA to withdraw the proposed rule.

P a g e  1 | 31 
 
Introduction 
 
On May 5, 2023, the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) published a proposed 
rule titled, “Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations.” 88 FR 
29496 (May 5, 2023). The proposal is intended to modify the Clean Water Act (CWA) 
water quality standards (WQS) program, promulgated at 40 CFR Part 131, to establish 
“baseline” water quality standards that would apply to all Indian reservation waters, as 
described in section 131.xx(a) of the proposed rule. WQS are the foundation for many 
other CWA programs, including impairment assessments, total maximum daily load 
(TMDL) development, section 401 certifications, effluent limits, and permitting decisions 
under the section 402 and section 404 discharge permit programs.   
 
The proposed rule is legally flawed and implicates many of the same legal and policy 
problems reflected in EPA’s recent proposed rule that would mandate states to establish 
designated uses for tribal reserved rights (87 FR 74361 (Dec. 5, 2022)). Both proposed 
rules exceed EPA’s authority under the CWA, implicate the “major questions” doctrine, 
create significant and unanswered implementation questions, and are inconsistent with 
recent Supreme Court decisions limiting agency authority, including Sackett v. EPA, 598 
U.S. ___ (2023)., Biden v. Nebraska, 600 U.S. ___ (2023), W. Virginia v. Env't Prot. 
Agency, 142 S. Ct. 2587 (2022). The proposed rule would also create a framework for 
establishing water quality criteria without any of the procedural requirements of the CWA, 
EPA’s regulations, and the Administrative Procedure Act (APA). As demonstrated in the 
comments below, EPA has failed to provide rationale or explanation for the proposal, 
failed to consider important aspects of the problem, and has proposed a rule that exceeds 
its statutory authority and is not in accordance with the law. 
 
The J.R. Simplot Company (Simplot) is a privately held, global agribusiness corporation 
based in Boise, Idaho. Simplot is engaged in a number of businesses including food 
processing, farming, fertilizer manufacturing, beef cattle feedlots, mining, ranching and 
other enterprises related to agriculture. Simplot has operations throughout the United 
States, with the majority of the facilities to the west of the Mississippi River Valley. As a 
part of the U.S. operations, Simplot has been a reliable domestic manufacturer and 
supplier of phosphate nutrients to American agriculture for over seven decades.  
Simplot’s operations are often subject to the requirements of the CWA, including section 
303 water quality standards and TMDLs; section 401 water quality certification; section 
311 oil spill prevention and response; section 404 dredge and fill permit; and section 402 
discharge permit programs; and, of course, every regulatory interaction with the CWA 
implicates the ever-changing regulatory definition of “waters of the United States” 
(WOTUS). Thus, this rulemaking is of interest as it has a direct impact on the company’s 
United States operations. 
 
 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  2 | 31 
 
I.  EPA’s Rationale for the Proposed Rule is Flawed  
 
The CWA and EPA regulations provide an opportunity for federally recognized tribes to 
obtain “treatment as a state” (referred to as TAS) authority under the CWA. TAS tribes 
can promulgate tribal WQS that, if approved by EPA, become effective for CWA 
purposes. TAS tribes can also obtain authority to administer other CWA programs, 
including the 401 certification and the 402 and 404 discharge permit programs. EPA 
states that its preference is for “Tribes to obtain TAS and develop WQS tailored to the 
Tribe’s individual environmental goals and reservation waters.” However, the proposed 
rule also states that “[a]t the current pace, it could take more than a decade for CWA-
effective WQS to be put in place for all Indian reservations”, indicating that EPA has 
developed this proposed rule to fast-track WQS development for Indian reservations. 88 
FR at 29499. Instead of devoting resources to promote tribal self-determination under the 
existing TAS framework, EPA is proposing its novel baseline WQS rule that would expand 
federal regulation over tribal reservation lands. This proposal has the potential to disrupt 
state and TAS tribal WQS programs and upend the cooperative federalism foundation of 
the CWA. EPA’s proposed rationale for the proposed rule cannot withstand scrutiny.  
 
As an initial matter, EPA asserts that following the existing CWA TAS process would 
“further delay needed water quality protections,” but the proposed rule lacks any 
explanation or description of this need. EPA has not cited specific waters on Indian 
reservations that are in need of protections, has not provided examples of EPA-issued 
NPDES permits that are not protective because a lack of tribal water quality standards, 
and EPA has not identified specific situations that demand greater federal regulatory 
control over tribal lands. It is not enough for EPA to simply state that there is a lack of 
water quality standards. To justify the proposed rule, EPA must demonstrate that there is 
an actual need for additional water quality protections, and that the proposed baseline 
standards and the associated increase in federal regulatory control on tribal lands would 
provide a remedy. 
 
Along the same lines, the proposal preamble appears to significantly underestimate the 
extent that tribal reservations are covered by CWA protections. The proposed rule 
preamble states that 574 tribes have been recognized by the federal government and that 
more than 300 of those tribes have “formal or informal” reservations. 88 FR at 29498. The 
proposed rule goes on to state that “only” 84 tribes with Indian reservations have applied 
for and received TAS authority under the CWA and, of these, “only 47 Tribes” have 
adopted or have submitted WQS for EPA approval. The proposal then states “EPA 
estimates that about 76,000 miles of rivers and streams and 1.9 million acres of lakes, 
reservoirs, and other open surface waters within Indian reservations currently lack CWA-
effective WQS…” 88 FR at 29499. Based on this summary, the proposal concludes that 
“…the majority of Indian reservations do not have this foundational protection laid out by 
Congress in the CWA for their waters.” Id. EPA’s conclusion is misleading and inaccurate 
with respect to the total reservation lands that currently have CWA protections.  
EPA’s statement that the majority of reservations currently lack CWA protections ignores 
the significant variation in both the size of reservations and the number of waters on those 
lands. As EPA well knows all tribes do not have equally sized reservation lands, or an 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  3 | 31 
 
equal number of waters that would be subject to the CWA. See Figure 1. A few tribes 
have very large reservation lands but the reservation lands for most tribes are relatively 
small. Many of the tribes with large reservation lands already have TAS authority and, of 
those, many have adopted WQS consistent with the CWA. When viewed on a land area 
basis, 72% of reservation lands in the United States belong to tribes with TAS authority 
and 75% of those reservation lands have CWA-effective WQS (Tables 1a and 1b). Thus, 
a key justification for the proposed rule, EPA’s assertion that a majority of tribal waters do 
not have TAS authority and do not have CWA-effective WQS, is inaccurate and 
misleading. Waters on the majority of reservation lands do have such protection, and 
nearly three quarters of reservation lands belong to tribes with TAS authority. 
 
Further to this point, the proposal explains that at the current pace of TAS applications, 
all tribes could have a CWA-based water quality standard program in ten years. 88 FR at 
29499. In other words, EPA could continue investing in TAS programming at its current 
rate and the remaining 28% of reservation lands could be covered by legitimate CWA 
TAS authority, with tribe specific WQS, in ten years. If EPA were able to increase its 
funding or programming for TAS efforts, it could likely reach that goal sooner. Given that 
EPA’s first unsuccessful attempt at a baseline WQS rulemaking was more than 20 years 
ago, and considering the significant legal vulnerability of the proposed rule (discussed in 
detail below), the agency’s only reasonable (and realistic) path forward is to focus agency 
efforts to achieve full implementation of the TAS program. EPA also fails to acknowledge 
that there is likely no scenario where EPA could “stand up,” let alone administer, the 
proposed baseline WQS program on 76,000 miles of rivers and streams or 1.9 million 
acres of open waters in less than 10 years.  
 
If the agency finalizes this proposed rule, it is likely to spend the next five or more years 
in court defending the rule and, in all likelihood, rewriting or revising some or all of it on 
court-ordered remand or vacatur. The only reasonable and lawful option is for EPA to 
continue promoting TAS and the associated tribal self-determination through the TAS 
program, and the CWA framework as Congress envisioned water quality protections on 
tribal lands would be established.

II.  The Proposed Rule is Unlawful 
 
A. EPA Does Not Have CWA Authority Over Tribal Lands Without TAS 
 
EPA claims its legal authority to propose the baseline WQS rule lies in an Administrator’s 
Determination that was made in 2001, pursuant to CWA section 303(c)(4)(B). EPA cannot 
rely on CWA section 303(c)(4)(B) because nothing in the CWA authorizes EPA to take 
oversight measures against tribes unless they have TAS authority. As a result, any 
Administrator’s Determination that claims authority over non-TAS reservation lands is 
beyond EPA’s authority and can have no legal effect. EPA does not have legal authority 
to promulgate the proposed baseline WQS regulation and, if finalized, the rule would be 
unlawful. 
 
The proposal correctly explains that CWA section 303 sets out detailed requirements for 
states to establish, maintain, and update water quality standards; to evaluate the quality 
of its waters against those standards; and to develop total maximum daily loads where 
waters are not meeting the applicable standards. The proposal fails to mention that 
section 303 does not mandate that tribes take any of these actions. Instead, CWA section 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  6 | 31 
 
518(e) provides tribes the opportunity to take on these CWA obligations, but nothing in 
the CWA requires tribes to do so.  
Pursuant to CWA section 518(e), EPA “is authorized to treat an Indian tribe as a State for 
purposes of [CWA] title II and [CWA] sections 104, 106, 303, 305, 308, 309, 314, 319, 
401, 402, 404 and 406 of this Act to the degree necessary to carry out the objectives of 
this section, but only if…the tribe receives TAS authority from EPA.” 33 U.S.C. § 1377(e) 
(emphasis added). Put another way, section 303 does not apply to tribes or tribal lands 
unless that tribe has obtained TAS authority from EPA, triggering EPA oversight authority.  
In direct conflict with the text and structure of the CWA, including sections 303 and 518(e), 
the proposed rule, would only apply on tribal reservation lands where there is no TAS 
authority. Congress did not grant EPA authority to regulate tribal reservation lands or 
waters unless those tribes obtain TAS authority under section 518(e). If the agency 
proceeds to finalize this rule it must provide an alternate and legitimate source of legal 
authority and, explain how section 518(e) does not entirely preclude this action. 
 
B. Section 303 Does Not Authorize EPA to Promulgate Nationally 
Applicable Water Quality Standards 
 
EPA’s proposed rule is well beyond the authority Congress provided in section 303 of the 
CWA, it disrupts the balance of power between states/TAS tribes and the federal 
government, and there is no “clear statement” that Congress intended EPA to assert such 
authority. 
Both the courts and Congress agree that States and TAS tribes have primary authority to 
establish WQS under section 303 of the CWA. See 33 U.S.C. § 1313(a), (c)(1), (c)(2)(A); 
see City of Arcadia v. U.S. Env't Prot. Agency, 411 F.3d 1103, 1106 (9th Cir. 2005) (“The 
CWA operates on a principle of cooperative federalism where states take the lead in 
setting [water quality standards].”); Natural Res. Def. Council, Inc. v. EPA, 16 F.3d 1395, 
1401 (4th Cir. 1993) (“While the states and EPA share duties in achieving [the CWA’s] 
goal, primary responsibility for establishing appropriate water quality standards is left to 
the states.  EPA sits in a reviewing capacity of the state-implemented standards, with 
approval and rejection powers only” (citations omitted).)  EPA’s role under CWA section 
303(c) is limited to exercising oversight of those state and TAS tribal programs and 
serving as a “back stop” when states and TAS tribes fail to submit water quality standards 
that comply with federal standards or otherwise violate the CWA. Gulf Restoration 
Network v. McCarthy, 783 F.3d 227, 231 (5th Cir. 2015). EPA has not provided any 
information, evidence, or legal analysis to support its claim that section 303(c)(4)(B) 
authorizes the action EPA proposes to take. 
 
 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  7 | 31 
 
Section 303(c)(4) provides EPA oversight authority to step in where a state or TAS tribe 
is not performing its obligations under the CWA. This is clear from the language of the 
statutory section, which states in its entirety: 
“The Administrator shall promptly prepare and publish proposed regulations 
setting forth a revised or new water quality standard for the navigable waters 
involved— 
(A) if a revised or new water quality standard submitted by such State under 
paragraph (3) of this subsection for such waters is determined by the Administrator 
not to be consistent with the applicable requirements of this Act, or 
(B) in any case where the Administrator determines that a revised or new standard 
is necessary to meet the requirements of this Act. The Administrator shall 
promulgate any revised or new standard under this paragraph not later than ninety 
days after he publishes such proposed standards, unless prior to such 
promulgation, such state has adopted a revised or new water quality 
standard which the Administrator determines to be in accordance with this 
Act.” 
33 U.S.C. § 1313(c)(4) (emphasis added). Under this section, EPA’s role is to ensure that 
water quality standards required by the CWA are established and are consistent with the 
CWA. Under subsection (A), if a state or TAS tribe promulgates a water quality standard 
that does not comply with the CWA, EPA can reject that state or tribal standard and 
establish a federal standard in its place. Under subsection (B), if a state or TAS tribe has 
not established a water quality standard where EPA believes one is necessary, EPA can 
establish a federal standard unless the state or TAS tribe acts first. Also, under 
subsection (B), EPA must finalize any proposed federal water quality standard within 90 
days of its proposal.1  
This interpretation is consistent with EPA’s own regulation, which clearly sets forth “State 
authority” in 40 CFR 131.4, and “EPA authority” in 40 CFR 131.5. Pursuant to section 
131.4, 
“States (as defined in 131.3) are responsible for reviewing, establishing, and 
revising water quality standards. As recognized by section 510 of the Clean Water 
Act, States may develop water quality standards more stringent than required by 
this regulation. Consistent with section 101(g) and 518(a) of the Clean Water Act, 
water quality standards shall not be construed to supersede or abrogate rights to 
quantities of water.”  
 
1 The timelines in the above-quoted statutory provision are further evidence that Congress in no way 
intended EPA to pursue nationally applicable water quality standards through this provision of the CWA. 
Under 303(c)(4)(B), EPA is statutorily mandated to finalize a federal WQS rule within 90 days after the 
proposed rule is published in the Federal Register. Here, the proposed baseline rule was published 
on May 5, 2023 and, if EPA were to comply with these mandates it would need to finalize this rule by 
August 3, 2023. EPA has not acknowledged this mandatory timeline, and has clearly made no effort to 
comply with it as the public comment period for the proposal doesn’t even close until August 3. It is clear 
that EPA has no intention of complying with the timelines Congress mandated for this rulemaking. EPA 
has not explained how it squares these mandatory timelines with its proposed action and why it does not 
believe these timelines apply to the proposed rule. 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  8 | 31 
 
(Emphasis added.) Pursuant to section 131.5,  
“EPA is to review and approve or disapprove State-adopted water quality 
standards... If EPA determines that the State or Tribe’s water quality standards are 
consistent with the factors listed in paragraphs (a)(1) through (8) of this section, 
EPA approves the standards. EPA must disapprove the State’s or Tribe’s water 
quality standards and promulgate Federal standards under section 303(c)(4) … if 
State or Tribal adopted standards are not consistent with the factors listed in 
paragraphs (a)(1) through (8) of this section. EPA may also promulgate a new or 
revised standard when necessary to meet the requirements of the Act.” 
(Emphasis added.) It is clear that when EPA promulgated these regulations (first in 1983 
and updated several times, most recently in 2015), it understood that states and 
authorized tribes are to establish water quality standards and that EPA’s role is one of 
oversight. Under its own regulations, EPA is only to promulgate water quality standards 
where a state or authorized tribe failed in its duties under the CWA.  
EPA’s longstanding guidance and its administration of the WQS program over the last 50 
years is also consistent with this interpretation. See e.g., U.S. EPA Water Quality 
Standards Handbook (2017), section 1.5 (“The CWA requires states and authorized tribes 
to adopt new or revised WQS and then submit them to the EPA for review and approval 
or disapproval action. The EPA has the authority and duty under CWA Section 303(c)(3) 
to approve or disapprove new or revised WQS.”); section 1.5.2 (“In the case of 
disapproval, the EPA must promptly propose federal WQS under Section 303(c)(4)(A) 
and promulgate such WQS within 90 days of their denial of the proposal, provided that 
the state or tribe does not make appropriate corrections within 90 days. The EPA may 
approve some provisions and not others within the same WQS submission.”); section 
1.5.2 (“The EPA may also promulgate a new or revised federal WQS where the 
Administrator determines under Section 303(c)(4)(B) that such a WQS is necessary to 
meet the requirements of the CWA, and no WQS have been submitted for the EPA to 
disapprove under Section 303(c)(4)(A). This situation could occur, for instance, if the EPA 
determines that particular WQS provisions are missing from a state’s or tribe’s currently 
applicable WQS or that the current WQS do not otherwise meet the requirements of the 
CWA.”). 
Nothing in section 303(c)(4) of the CWA, in EPA’s regulations, or in its longstanding 
interpretive guidance or implementation actions could have predicted that EPA would take 
the extraordinary and unprecedented step of establishing “baseline” water quality 
standards purporting to be applicable on all tribal reservation lands. After more than five 
decades administering the CWA, EPA’s proposal for the first time “‘claimed to discover in 
a long-extant statute an unheralded power’ representing a ‘transformative expansion in 
its regulatory authority.’” W. Virginia v. Env't Prot. Agency, 142 S. Ct. 2587, 2610 (2022). 
EPA’s proposed reliance on section 303(c)(4)(B) to expand its authority to promulgate 
nationally applicable water quality standards is similar to the Agency’s unlawful location 
of “newfound power in the vague language of an ancillary provision of the Act” that the 
Supreme Court rejected in W. Virginia. Id. Indeed, EPA’s new interpretation of section 
303(c)(4)(B) would allow it to promulgate nationally applicable water quality standards 
outside of its oversight role, an authority that could be used to undermine or even usurp 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  9 | 31 
 
established state and TAS tribal authority in their own water quality standards programs. 
EPA’s new interpretation would upend the cooperative federalism foundation of the CWA. 
The Supreme Court recently rejected similar agency actions, see, e.g., Nat’l Federation 
of Indep. Bus. v. OSHA, 142 S.Ct. 661, 665(2022) (per curiam); Alabama Ass’n. of 
Realtors v. DHS, 141 S.Ct. 2485, 2487 (2021) (per curiam); W. Virginia, 142 S. Ct. at 
2615-16, and recently reiterated that an agency must “point to clear congressional 
authorization” when it asserts extraordinary authorities. Nebraska v. Biden, 600 U.S. 
_____ (Slip. Op. 2023), citing W. Virginia, 142 S. Ct. at 2609 (2022).   
Relatedly, the proposed rule does not pass muster under Sackett v. EPA, 598 U.S. ___, 
143 S. Ct. 1322 (May 25, 2023) or Solid Waste Agency of Northern Cook Cty. v. Army 
Corps of Engineers, 531 U.S. 159, 168 (2001) (SWANCC). In those cases, EPA and the 
Army Corps of Engineers interpreted a provision of the CWA in a way that would 
“significantly alter the balance between federal and state power and the power of the 
Government over private property” (Sackett, 143 S. Ct. at 1341 ) and that “would result in 
a significant impingement of the States’ traditional and primary power over land and water 
use.” (SWANCC at 174). The Supreme Court explained that “[w]here an administrative 
interpretation of a statute invokes the outer limits of Congress’ power, we expect a clear 
indication that Congress intended that result,” id. at 172, especially “where the 
administrative interpretation alters the federal-state framework by permitting federal 
encroachment upon a traditional state power.” Id. at 173. As Congress had not provided 
any such clear statement, the Court “reject[ed] the [Agencies’] request for administrative 
deference” to their claim of jurisdiction. Id. at 174. Similarly, in this case, there is no 
evidence, much less clear evidence, that Congress intended to grant EPA the power to 
establish nationally applicable WQS that can override state and TAS tribal standards, let 
alone establish procedures for itself that do not meet the minimum federal standards (see 
discussion below). 
If Congress had intended to authorize EPA to establish and administer nationally 
applicable federal water quality standards in section 303(c)(4), it would have said so. 
Congress knows how to direct EPA to make nationally applicable regulations, and it did 
so explicitly in CWA section 304(b), directing EPA to develop nationally applicable 
technology-based federal effluent limitation guidelines for specific industries; and CWA 
section 304(a), directing EPA to develop recommended water quality standards that are 
based on the latest scientific information and that can be adopted through rulemaking by 
states and TAS tribes. But Congress did not authorize EPA to establish nationally 
applicable water quality standards in section 303(c)(4), and Congress certainly did not 
intend for EPA to backdoor itself into that role. Nothing in section 303(c)(4) authorizes 
EPA to promulgate nationwide water quality standards and the proposed rule, if finalized, 
would be an unlawful overreach of EPA authority.  
Lastly, this is not an area where EPA can claim deference for its interpretation, because 
Congress has spoken directly to the question of the assignment of authority to states and 
TAS tribes to set designated uses and water quality criteria. Chevron, U.S.A. v. Nat. Res. 
Def. Council, 467 U.S. 837, 842 (1984) (“First, always, is the question whether Congress 
has directly spoken to the precise question at issue. If the intent of Congress is clear, that 
is the end of the matter; for the court, as well as the agency, must give effect to the 
unambiguously expressed intent of Congress”). EPA’s attempt to create new authority for 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  10 | 31 
 
itself that Congress provided only to states and TAS tribes should be accorded no 
deference.   
For all of the reasons detailed above, the proposed rule violates the CWA and the APA 
and easily implicates the “major questions” doctrine. Simplot respectfully requests EPA 
withdraw the proposal, develop additional information and materials for public review, and 
meaningfully engage with states and stakeholders on this important topic. 
 
 
C. The Proposed Designated Uses Are Inconsistent with the CWA and 
Unlawful  
 
Under the Clean Water Act framework, states and TAS tribes are required to establish 
water quality standards that “shall consist of the designated uses of the navigable waters 
involved and the water quality criteria for such waters based upon such uses.” 33 U.S.C. 
§ 1313(c). Water quality criteria “support a particular use” and when the criteria are met 
“water quality will generally protect the designated use.” 40 CFR § 131.3 (b). Designated 
uses are “those uses specified in water quality standards for each water body or segment 
whether or not they are being attained.” 40 CFR § 131.3 (f). In other words, designated 
uses are specific uses identified for specific water bodies, or segments of water bodies.  
 
The proposed rule would establish a single set of designated uses for waterbodies located 
across the country in different climates, at different elevations, in different communities, 
and that likely have very divergent uses. This is not only inappropriate from a water 
resource management perspective, but also inconsistent with the CWA. Designated uses 
are incorporated into water quality standards so that the particular use of the particular 
waters or segment of waters involved will be protected by the applicable criteria. Often, 
a single water body or segment may have multiple designated uses that are targeted for 
the actual uses of that water for segment. See e.g., U.S. EPA Water Quality Standards 
Handbook (2017); EPA Watershed Academy Web: Introduction to the Clean Water Act; 
Water Quality Criteria.2 The proposal does not describe the types of waters the baseline 
standards would apply to, nor does it even suggest that EPA knows how these waters 
are actually used.  
 
For example, the proposal generically explains that cultural and traditional uses are 
important to tribes, that protecting tribal cultural uses also protects tribal welfare, and then 
summarily concludes that the proposed cultural and traditional designated use is 
consistent the CWA. 88 FR at 29503. The problem is, as EPA acknowledges, cultural and 
traditional tribal uses “can include a variety of uses specific to the ceremonies and 
traditions of each Tribe, and each use may require different levels of protection.” 88 
FR at 29503 (emphasis added). The very tribe-specific nature of cultural and traditional 
practices precludes EPA from even attempting to define the term. If EPA cannot define 
the term, and EPA acknowledges that different such uses across the country may require 
different levels of protection, it stands to reason that the agency cannot reasonably or 
lawfully establish nationally applicable standards to protect those widely varying uses.   
 
2 Available at https://cfpub.epa.gov/watertrain/moduleFrame.cfm?parent_object_id=2003 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  11 | 31 
 
 
The reality is, the range of waters, characteristics and qualities, and the diversity of uses 
make it impossible to set effective nationally applicable water quality standards. EPA 
admits as much when it identifies the risk of establishing a nationwide public water supply 
designated use on all tribal reservation waters because it could result in “a designation 
on a water body where such a use is not appropriate (e.g., waters that may not have 
enough flow to support public water supply uses).” 88 FR at 29503. This is the very reason 
why all of the proposed designated uses are flawed. And, this is why, despite what the 
proposed rule says, it does not actually set nationally applicable water quality criteria. 
Rather, as discussed further below, the proposal establishes placeholder narrative criteria 
and a framework that authorizes EPA to set the real CWA-applicable numeric water 
quality criteria in the future without any public comment or rulemaking process. This type 
of framework is not authorized by the CWA and if finalized would be unlawful.  
 
 
D. The Proposed “Cultural and Traditional Use” Is Not Consistent with 
the CWA 
 
When establishing water quality standards, including designated uses, EPA is required 
to comply with all federal regulations, policies and procedures that apply to the 
development and approval of state water quality standards. 40 CFR § 131.22(c). The 
proposed rule does not meet these minimum federal requirements.  
 
Federal regulations require states, and in this case requires EPA, to “specify appropriate 
water uses to be achieved and protected.” 40 CFR § 131.10(a). The proposed “cultural 
and traditional uses” designated use does not specify any actual uses that are to be 
achieved or protected. The proposal indicates that this use could encompass anything 
from full physical submersion for water rituals, to the protection of aquatic grass used in 
basket weaving, to hunting for land-based game species. 88 CFR at 29503. A lawful 
“cultural and traditional uses” designated use cannot be established on a nationwide basis 
because EPA has admitted that it does not know, and therefore cannot “specify” what 
types of cultural uses tribal waters are subject to, and therefore cannot meet the 
requirement in 40 CFR § 131.10(a). Id.  
 
Based on the proposed rule, EPA would apply the cultural and traditional use designation 
universally to all waters within the boundary of each individual reservation without 
considering whether the designated use is necessary for the protection of how the waters 
are actually being used. The proposal cites the basis for this as being consistent with 
CWA section 101(a)(2) which must provide baseline water quality for the “protection and 
propagation of fish, shellfish, and wildlife, and provide for recreation in and on the water.” 
This CWA goal has been more commonly referred to as meeting “fishable/swimmable” 
objectives and has been implemented through the aquatic life and recreational 
designated uses. It is recognized that tribal uses of waters are unique and may extend 
beyond the protections needed to ensure attainment of fishable or swimmable uses; 
however, if the purpose of this rule is to establish “Federal Baseline Water Quality 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  12 | 31 
 
Standards for Indian Reservations” including a cultural and traditional use as part of 
baseline water quality standards is unnecessary and inconsistent with the CWA.  
 
It is also uncertain if the cultural and traditional designated use can be considered an 
“existing use” universally in all waters on tribal reservation lands. As defined 40 CFR § 
131.3 existing uses are those uses that have been “actually attained in the water body on 
or after November 28, 1975.” EPA presents no information or evidence that even the 
majority of waters on all tribal reservation lands have been used for cultural and traditional 
practices on or after November 28, 1975. Where there is no cultural and traditional 
existing use, the application of this “baseline” designated use would result in more 
stringent water quality criteria than is necessary to protect the existing uses or reasonably 
anticipated uses that could be attained in the future. The distinction of existing and 
attained uses since 1975 is important because many waters in the U.S. historically would 
have been used for subsistence fishing at some time in history; however, the CWA is 
explicit in not defaulting to historic uses because it has not been necessary in more recent 
times to provide such protection. 
State and TAS tribal water quality standards are further required to be protective of the 
designated use, “based on sound scientific rationale,” and to “contain sufficient 
parameters or constituents to protect the designated use.” 40 CFR § 131.11(a)(1). As a 
threshold matter, if EPA cannot define the designated use, it cannot promulgate a 
standard that is protective of that use. Additionally, EPA has presented no information or 
evidence that the proposed narrative standards and binding translations are based on 
sound science or that they contain sufficient parameters to protect the proposed 
designated uses. Indeed, because the actual binding and CWA applicable water quality 
criteria will be a future translation of the narrative language in the proposed rule, and the 
translation could be based on several different options provided in the proposal, there is 
no way for EPA to demonstrate that the future criteria would be based on sound science 
or protective of the designated uses.  
 
Lastly, EPA’s failure to define “cultural and traditional uses,” indeed its refusal to do so, 
renders the rule too vague to understand and implement, and therefore does not meet 
the regulatory requirements that apply to all water quality standards. The proposed rule 
and the designated uses are arbitrary and capricious and, if finalized, would be unlawful. 
 
 
E. The Proposed “Binding Translation” Framework is Unlawful 
 
The proposed rule purports to establish narrative water quality criteria, but the actual 
applicable CWA water quality criteria that will be used to develop permit discharge limits, 
evaluate waters for impairments, and that will be used to establish TMDLs, will be 
established later by EPA without any public procedures. Proposed 131.XX (d)(2). This is 
contrary to the CWA, EPA’s existing regulations and the APA and, if finalized as 
proposed, will be unlawful.  

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  13 | 31 
 
Among other things, state water quality standards are to be set after states follow 
“applicable legal procedures for revising or adopting standards.” 40 CFR §131.5(a)(6). 
EPA is subject to these same requirements. 40 CFR § 131.22(c). For EPA, applicable 
legal procedures include notice and comment rulemaking in accordance with the APA. 5 
U.S.C. §553.  
 
The proposed rule presents for public comment a suite of narrative water quality criteria. 
But the proposed narrative criteria will not be the applicable criteria for CWA purposes. In 
fact, the proposed rule effectively prohibits the EPA Regional Administrator from 
administering the narrative criteria for CWA purposes and requires that they first be 
translated into numeric criteria before they are used for CWA purposes. Proposed 
131.XX(d) (“The Regional Administrator shall utilize one of the options set forth in this 
paragraph (d)(2) to derive numeric translations of the narrative criteria in paragraph (d)(1) 
of this section for all purposes under the [CWA] section 303(c) for specific parameters 
as necessary to protect applicable designated uses…”). It is clear that the proposed 
narrative standards presented for public comment are merely a stalking horse and will 
have no effect for CWA purposes. Any new numeric water quality criteria established 
through the “binding translation” procedures EPA proposes, contrary to its obligations 
under the CWA and the APA, would be unlawful.  
 
 
 
III. The “Binding Translation” Options are Unworkable  
 
The proposal includes five options for EPA Regional Administrators to establish the 
applicable numeric criteria via “binding translations.” In addition to being an unlawful 
procedure to establish applicable criteria for CWA purposes, the translation options 
themselves are seriously flawed.  
A. Translation options 1, 2, and 3 are Unworkable and Result in 
Significant Uncertainty for the Regulated Community. 
 
Under translation option 1, the EPA Regional Administrator can simply replace the 
promulgated narrative criteria with EPA’s CWA section 304(a) recommended criteria. 
Proposed 131.XX(d)(2)(i). In section 304(a) Congress directed EPA to develop and 
publish information and recommended water quality criteria “accurately reflecting the 
latest scientific knowledge.” 33 U.S.C. 1314(a). The 304(a) criteria are provided to states 
and TAS tribes as recommendations, but they are not self-implementing. If a state or TAS 
tribe adopts EPA’s 304(a) criteria, it must go through the same “applicable legal 
procedures for revising or adopting standards.” 40 CFR §131.5(a)(6). No state or TAS 
tribe would be authorized to simply adopt EPA’s 304(a) criteria through guidance or some 
other non-public process; and yet, EPA proposes that authority for itself.  

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  14 | 31 
 
Under translation option 2, the narrative criteria can be replaced with EPA’s section 
304(a) recommended criteria but “modified to reflect site-specific conditions and aquatic 
communities based on sound scientific rationale…incorporating where relevant: 
Indigenous Knowledge, often referred to as Traditional Ecological Knowledge, as 
appropriate…” Proposed 131.XX(d)(2)(ii) (emphasis added). When a state or TAS tribe 
modifies 304(a) recommended criteria with state- or site-specific information, EPA 
requires those criteria to go through public rulemaking procedures and be subject to EPA 
approval. The process for developing state- or site-specific information typically takes 
years and significant engagement with the public and stakeholders to ensure that the 
information and data being used is accurate and reflects site-specific conditions. The 
proposal would have EPA Regional Administrators undertake this process from start to 
finish with no public input, no required engagement with the public, and no rulemaking 
procedures. In other words, EPA Regional Administrators will have no accountability for 
the information they use or the decisions they make to modify 304(a) criteria to be tribe 
specific. 
 
Similarly, under translation option 3, the narrative criteria can be replaced with some other 
“WQS adopted by the Tribe but not yet CWA effective or applicable CWA-effective WQS 
in adjacent or other relevant state or Tribe that are based on sound scientific rationale, 
reflect similar waterbody characteristics, and ensure protection of the applicable 
designated uses established under this rule, taking into consideration Indigenous 
Knowledge, as appropriate.” Proposed 131.XX(d)(2)(iii) (emphasis added). This is clearly 
an unlawful work-around of the TAS program that Congress created specifically to 
authorize tribes to establish WQS for CWA purposes. In option 3, EPA proposes that it 
could replace the narrative criteria it is presenting for public comment with a non-TAS, 
non-CWA WQS developed by a tribe with potentially no public procedure at all. This 
clearly violates the CWA, including the procedural requirements for tribes to obtain TAS 
before establishing WQS for CWA purposes. 33 U.S.C. § 1377(e); 40 CFR § 131.8. 
Additionally, an inconsistency is present throughout these options. WQS developed under 
these options are required to be based on sound scientific rationale and must be 
scientifically defensible under the CWA; however, they could also be based on Indigenous 
Knowledge. The proposal does not define the term Indigenous Knowledge or explain how 
it would or could be used to set numeric criteria that is consistent with the CWA’s rigorous 
scientific standards.  
The CWA requires EPA to meet the following scientific standards and obligations when 
developing water quality standards3:  
• Section 104 (to conduct and coordinate research, investigations, 
experiments, training, demonstrations, surveys, and studies relating to the 
causes, effects, extent, prevention, reduction, and elimination of pollution; 
to develop and issue to states the “latest scientific knowledge available” 
regarding the presence of pesticides in water); 
 
3 See 33. U.S.C. §§ 1254, 1267, 1268, 1269, 1270, 1273, and 1314. 

Proposed Indian Land WQS:  Comments 
 
P a g e  15 | 31 
 
• Sections 117, 118, 119, 120, 121 (to make scientific inquiry into water 
quality concerns in specific waters and watersheds, (e.g. Chesapeake Bay, 
Great Lakes, Long Island Sound); and 
• Section 304 (to develop, and from time to time thereafter revise, criteria 
for water quality “accurately reflecting the latest scientific knowledge…on 
the kind and extent of all identifiable effects on health and welfare…on the 
concentration and dispersal of pollutants, or their byproducts, through 
biological, physical, and chemical processes…on the effects of pollutants 
on biological community diversity, productivity, and stability…”) 
To implement these Congressional mandates, EPA promulgated regulations that require 
water quality standards be based on "sound scientific rationale" and "scientifically 
defensible methods.”4 
Neither the CWA nor EPA’s regulations define the terms “sound scientific rationale” or 
“scientifically defensible methods.” However, EPA has made clear that water quality 
standards should be based on data and scientific information derived from biological, 
chemical, and/or toxicological studies published in peer-reviewed literature with a focus 
on science, science policy, and risk management. The following examples from EPA 
guidance demonstrate that the foundation for water quality standards must be science-
based: 
• “When EPA speaks of science or scientific analysis, it is referring to the 
extraction of data from toxicological or exposure studies and surveys with a 
minimum of judgment being used to make inferences from the available 
evidence.” (EPA Methodology for Deriving Ambient Water Quality Criteria 
for the Protection of Human Health, EPA 2000.5) 
•  “Water quality criteria developed under section 304(a) are based solely on 
data and scientific judgments on the relationship between pollutant 
concentrations and environmental and human health effects. Section 
304(a) criteria do not reflect consideration of economic impacts or the 
technological feasibility of meeting the chemical concentrations in ambient 
water.” (National Recommended Water Quality Criteria Recommendations, 
EPA 2002.6) 
• “EPA’s national recommended water quality criteria are scientifically 
derived numeric values that EPA determines will generally protect aquatic 
life or human health from the adverse effects of pollutants in ambient water.” 
(Final Updated Ambient Water Quality Criteria for the Protection of Human 
Health, 80 FR 36986.)  
Additionally, CWA section 311 defines the term “best available science” to mean “science 
that (A) maximizes the quality, objectivity, and integrity of information, including statistical 
information; (B) uses peer-reviewed and publicly available data; and (C) clearly 
documents and communicates risks and uncertainties in the scientific basis for such
