Dear Mr. Ray:  
  
These comments are submitted on behalf of the Wisconsin Paper Council (WPC), 
Wisconsin Manufacturers and Commerce (WMC), and the Midwest Food Products 
Association (MWFPA).  
WPC is the premier trade association in Wisconsin that advocates for the papermaking 
industry before regulatory bodies, and state and federal legislatures to achieve positive 
policy outcomes. WPC also works to educate the public about the social, environmental, 
and economic importance of paper, pulp, and forestry production in Wisconsin and 
throughout the Midwest. The pulp and paper sector employs over 30,000 people in 
Wisconsin and has an annual payroll of $2.5 billion. Wisconsin is the number one paper-
producing state in the United States, with the output of paper manufactured products 
estimated to be over $18 billion.  
WMC is the largest general business association in Wisconsin, representing 
approximately 3,800 member companies of all sizes, and from every sector of the 
economy. Since 1911, WMC’s mission has been to make Wisconsin the most competitive 
state in the nation to do business. WMC members depend on fair and predictable 
environmental standards that do not unduly target or harm Wisconsin businesses.  
MWFPA is a trade association founded in 1905 representing the food processing industry 
in the states of Wisconsin, Minnesota, and Illinois. MWFPA’s purpose includes advocating 
on public policy issues including food safety, workforce, and environmental regulations. 

 
2  
  
Wisconsin has been delegated authority by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) 
pursuant to the Clean Water Act to issue wastewater discharge permits. The Wisconsin 
Department of Natural Resources (DNR) issues these permits pursuant to the Wisconsin 
Pollutant Discharge Elimination (WPDES) permit program. Many or our members operate 
in conformance with WPDES permits, which include provisions for compliance with 
applicable water quality criteria. 
EPA is proposing to create federal baseline water quality standards (WQS), with certain 
exceptions, for Indian reservation waters that currently do not have Clean Water Act 
(CWA) water quality standards. WQS include designated uses, water quality criteria, and 
antidegradation policies. EPA notes, however, that “Tribes are encouraged to seek 
authority to administer their own WQS program under the [Clean Water] Act’s provisions 
for eligible Tribes to be treated in a similar manner as states (TAS authority).” EPA’s 
proposed baseline WQS would not apply to where Tribes with TAS authority have EPA-
approved WQS.  
 
Nationally, EPA notes that this rule would apply to more than 250 reservations, containing 
approximately 76,000 of rivers and streams, and about 1.9 million acres of lakes and other 
surface waters.  
 
Wisconsin has eleven federally recognized tribes. These include:  
 
• 
Bad River Band of Lake Superior Chippewa 
• 
Ho-Chunk Nation 
• 
Lac Courte Oreilles Band of Lake Superior Chippewa 
• 
Lac du Flambeau Band of Lake Superior Chippewa 
• 
Menominee Tribe of Wisconsin 
• 
Oneida Nation 
• 
Forest County Potawatomi 
• 
Red Cliff Band of Lake Superior Chippewa 
• 
St. Croix Chippewa 
• 
Sokaogon Chippewa (Mole Lake) 
• 
Stockbridge-Munsee 
 
According to EPA’s website, the Bad River Band of Lake Superior Chippewa, the Lac du  
Flambeau Band of Lake Superior Chippewa and the Sokaogon Chippewa Community 
have TAS authority and have EPA-approved WQS (see EPA Actions on Tribal Water 
Quality Standards and Contacts | US EPA). Thus, EPA’s proposed rule would not apply 
to these Tribes. The baseline standards, however, would apply to other Tribes in 
Wisconsin. Thus, this rule will have a significant impact on Wisconsin Tribes and other 
entities.  
 
In fact, EPA notes that WQS would be used to develop discharge limits for facilities that 
discharge into or upstream of tribal waters. EPA specifically lists “industries discharging 
pollutants that may affect surface waters on Indian reservations currently without CWA-
effective WQS” as potentially impacted entities. In addition, WQS are also used to assess 

 
3  
  
water quality, identify impaired waters and develop total maximum daily loads for impaired 
waters.  
 
WPC, WMC, and MWFPA urge EPA to withdraw this proposed rule. EPA lacks the 
necessary authority under CWA to promulgate this rule. Moreover, the proposed 
regulatory approach could result in unattainable water quality standards, would impose 
far greater costs than estimated by EPA, and could create incredible regulatory 
uncertainty for Wisconsin manufacturers and businesses.  
 
In addition, our coalition supports the joint comments submitted on this docket by the U.S. 
Chamber of Commerce, American Forest and Paper Association, and several other 
organizations, and the comments of the Federal Water Quality Coalition and hereby 
incorporates both those sets of comments herein by reference.

RE: Snoqualmie Indian Tribe's Comments on Proposed EPA Baseline Tribal Water Quality 
Standards, Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405 
Dear Office of Water: 
The Snoqualmie Indian Tribe ("Tribe") is a federally-recognized sovereign Indian tribe with inherent 
sovereign rights and reserved rights as a signatory to the Treaty of Point Elliott of 1855, art. V, 12 
Stat. 928. The Tribe submits these comments on the EPA's proposed baseline tribal water quality 
standards. 
The Tribe supports the EPA's desire to formally protect tribal waters, consistent with legal 
obligations. It is appropriate and appreciated that the EPA baseline tribal water quality standards 
rule commits EPA to consult with the Tribe to establish human health and environmental objectives, 
and to protect culturally significant waters. The Tribe will directly benefit from EPA's position that all 
upstream water users must maintain a level of water quality that ensures the downstream tribal 
water quality standards are attained when waters enter the Snoqualmie Indian Reservation. 
However, the EPA rule should not inhibit the Tribe's determination of water quality under its own 
sovereign authorities. 
Free, prior and informed consent is a prerequisite 
The rule requires that EPA consult with the Tribe throughout its process of crafting standards; 
however, consultation is not a substitute for consent. EPA must not adopt or enforce baseline water 
quality standards affecting the Tribe, its land or resources without the Tribe's free, prior and 
informed consent ("FPIC"). 
FPIC is an international rights-based norm codified in United Nations Declaration of the Rights of 
Indigenous People ("UN DRIP") that governs interactions with indigenous peoples. FPIC stands for 
the principle that all project-affected indigenous peoples have the legal right to be part of decision­
making processes for projects that impact their lands and resources. FPIC is designed to replace 
historical processes that excluded indigenous peoples from decision-making regarding activities that 
occurred on or near their ancestral lands and resources. The United Nations General Assembly

re: Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations. Proposed rule. 
Docket No. EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405 
 
Dear Administrator Regan, 
The Petroleum Association of Wyoming represents companies involved in all aspects of responsible oil 
and natural gas development in Wyoming, including upstream production, oilfield services, midstream 
processing, pipeline transportation and essential work such as legal services, accounting, consulting and 
more1. PAW urges the Environmental Protection Agency to pursue development of water quality 
standards for Indian reservations in a more deliberate manner. The Clean Water Act (CWA) offers other 
avenues that, from the outset, will tailor standards more applicable to the waters in question and lessen 
the likelihood of severe variations in standards when crossing jurisdictional boundaries.  
The EPA is proposing to set water quality standards (WQS) based on narrative criteria and then go 
through a process to translate those narratives into numeric thresholds. The EPA’s proposed approach 
intends to set a static standard across each EPA region and then, only after the fact and through 
cumbersome processes, allow opportunities for adjustments. There is no "one size fits all" approach to 
surface water quality standards. Aquatic communities, hydrology, geologic formations, and 
anthropogenic influences vary substantially among surface waters. As a result, surface water quality 
standards should be tailored to site-specific circumstances and should be considered carefully in the 
context of individual reservations, individual waters and available resources for implementation. 
PAW believes the EPA would be better suited to start this process off right and set standards more 
nuanced to the needs of the water body. The EPA can accomplish this by tiering tribal WQS from those 
set by a state. States are often the primary regulator in water quality matters and have the depth of 
 
1 PAW advocates for oil and gas development that supports sustainable production of Wyoming’s abundant resources; fosters 
mutually beneficial relationships with Wyoming’s landowners, businesses, and communities; and upholds the values of 
science-based, environmental stewardship. Recent studies show this industry supports over 20,000 jobs in Wyoming and 
contributes $8 billion in economic activity. Our industry generates significant tax revenue to the state of Wyoming, funding 
state and local government operations, education and public infrastructure. Eighty-five percent of the oil and gas companies 
operating in Wyoming are classified as small businesses. 

expertise to appropriately set standards for each water body under consideration. States go through a 
laborious process to identify existing uses and to establish standards protective of those uses. Through 
the triennial review process, those standards and their attainment are under constant supervision.  
Another avenue available to the EPA is through Section 518(d) of the CWA. It states that "In order to 
ensure the consistent implementation of the requirements of this Act, an Indian tribe and the State or 
States in which the lands of such tribe are located may enter into a cooperative agreement, subject to the 
review and approval of the Administrator, to jointly plan and administer the requirements of this Act." 
Rather than promulgating broad federal baseline WQS for reservations, the EPA should consider 
encouraging tribes to enter into cooperative agreements with states to expand coverage over reservation 
waters. Again, adjacent states are much more likely to have WQS that take into consideration site-
specific factors that are critical to scientifically defensible WQS. 
The Wind River Indian Reservation is the only reservation in the nation home to two federally-
recognized tribes. There are many instances where having two sets of standards covering one land is not 
possible. Establishing WQS is such an instance. The tribes are no strangers to working through their 
differences to find common ground. But the process to get there is not always an easy one.  
PAW offers this for the EPA’s consideration in light of the EPA’s proposed intention to establish new 
uses, including for cultural and traditional factors. The EPA has not provided any indication of the level 
of protection that could be afforded for those uses. It is unclear how these new uses will interplay with 
compliance objectives for point sources in another jurisdiction upstream from an Indian reservation. And 
it is unclear how the EPA would resolve any potential differences of opinion in the level of protection 
sought. 
Though already set in regulation2, PAW recommends the EPA reconsider its position of carte blanche 
authority to deny a state-issued permit to which it has objected. PAW requests the EPA include an 
appeal process. States are delegated the authority to administer their programs upon approval from the 
EPA. In instances of disagreement and in line with cooperative federalism principles, the EPA should 
afford states greater latitude in making permitting decisions and offer an appeal process in those rare 
instances where amicable resolutions cannot be found. 
A final point of concern is with respect to the EPA’s proposed process for designation of Outstanding 
National Resource Waters (ONWR). PAW isn’t concerned about the ability for ONRW’s to be proposed 
or designated. But as currently written, the process of review and potential designation lacks the 
stringency commensurate with the protections applied to ONRW’s. State programs have established 
processes to review waters proposed for ONRW. They include thorough internal review and substantial 
opportunity for public and stakeholder input. The EPA’s proposed process seems to lack, or severely 
limit, these protocols. The standards applied to ONRW’s are the most stringent available for any 
waterbody, allowing for no new and time-limited increases in current point source discharges. This is 
true for both the water designated as an ONRW and for all of its tributaries. For a reservation like the 
WRIR, the Wind River traverses from one side to the other and nearly every navigable water is a 
 
2 40 CFR 122.4(c) 

tributary to it. The potential of it being designated as an ONRW, without truly strong standards for 
review, would have adverse effects to many industries on the WRIR. 
PAW appreciates the EPA’s consideration of our comments. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Sincerely, 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Colin McKee 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Regulatory Affairs Director 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Petroleum Association of Wyoming

Dear Administrator Regan, Assistant Administrator Fox, and Regional Administrator Sixkiller: 
 
Earlier today, the Alaska Department of Environmental Conservation (“DEC”) met with 
representatives of the Environmental Protection Agency (“EPA”) to discuss Alaska’s Human Health 
Criteria efforts, a State-led rulemaking potentially intersecting with EPA’s draft Tribal Reserved 
Rights Rule and EPA’s draft Tribal Baseline Water Quality Standards Rule.  Each of these 
rulemakings has important policy implications for Alaska.  EPA, however, has been mum at best, 
Machiavellian at worst, about how their new rules apply in our Great State.  In this meeting, we 
sought clarity.  Predictability.  We asked for something in writing.  Anything!  We needed some 
indication that EPA, indeed, is a partner to us in our efforts to protect the environment as we, the 
State, marry our environmental-protection policy objectives with our other policy goals.  But EPA 
indicated an alarming lack of understanding of Alaska and a severely distorted perception of the 
proper role of a federal agency in a democratic republic.   
 
Therefore, I write to express the State of Alaska’s continuing frustration with EPA’s lack of 
communication, transparency, and respect for the State.  The Clean Water Act calls for a 
cooperative federalism approach, which means “a partnership between the States and the Federal 
Government”1 where States take a “primary”2 role and EPA, a “secondary.”3  After over two years 
of interaction with the Biden EPA, however, EPA’s worldview is clear: EPA gets to play Congress,4 
crafting rules of national applicability and leaving the details to be filled in later.  And the Great State 
of Alaska?  We’re a puppet, a servant, subordinate to EPA.  Alaska exists to do EPA’s bidding, on 
EPA’s terms, per EPA’s rules—uninformed by Alaska’s input, experience, or policy preferences.

EPA engages with Alaska belatedly5 or not at all6,7 and speaks in the language of threats,8 mandates,9

In short?  We’re the sub to EPA’s Dom.   
 
Wildly inappropriate. 
 
Disturbingly, EPA’s latest rule (Tribal Baselines) is only the latest in a chain of denigrating 
actions taken by EPA.  EPA is now proposing to promulgate new water quality standards for all 
“reservations,” a term whose new definition is inconsistent with the Clean Water Act.  (Specifically, 
EPA disregards the Clean Water Act’s definition of “reservation” (“all land within the limits of an 
Indian reservation under the jurisdiction of the United States Government . . .”) in favor of its own 
(includes “formal and informal reservations such as trust land that has been validly set apart for use 
by a Tribe even if such land is located outside of the exterior boundaries of a formally designated 
reservation), creating ambiguity as well as legal issues.)13  Under this new proposal, permits for 
upstream discharges must have effluent limitations stringent enough to meet EPA’s new standards.14  
Alaska, through our APDES program, is the entity responsible for permitting upstream discharges.  
This means that Alaska will have to ensure that standards in upstream permits are protective of 
downstream “reservation” waters.  Considering Alaska’s 227 federally recognized Tribes, and EPA’s 
new, more expansive definition of “reservation,” this rule could drastically alter the permitting 
landscape in Alaska.   
  
Alaska’s water quality standards already apply throughout the State.15  Tribes who meet 
Congress criteria for Treatment as States (“TAS”) may promulgate their own.  When States (and 
TAS Tribes) make these types of decisions, they remain mindful of the bigger picture: the stricter the 
effluent limitations, the more costly the compliance, and the greater the disincentive for

development.  Alaska takes seriously its commitment to the health of its people, its environment,

responsible development while protecting our environment—reflect our commitment to Alaskans.  
 
Juggling multiple policy considerations, of course, is not EPA’s job.  This is why the Clean 
Water Act “recognize[s], preserve[s], and protect[s] the primary responsibilities and rights of 
States”—not EPA—to “prevent, reduce, and eliminate pollution,” and to “plan the development 
and use . . . of land and water resources.”  The United States Supreme Court now recognizes this 
too.17  States are well-equipped to balance the multiple policy considerations going into pollution 
control—it’s what we do.  EPA’s role, in contrast, is to “provide Federal technical services and 
financial aid to State and interstate agencies and municipalities in connection with the prevention, 
reduction, and elimination of pollution.”18  In other words, per the Clean Water Act and the United 
States Supreme Court: States make the policy calls, EPA provides technical and financial support.  
And where EPA exercises power?  States have the “right . . . to consult with the Administrator in 
[its] exercise.”19   
 
This broader framework is reflected in the Clean Water Act’s water quality standard 
provisions.  The Clean Water Act says that States and TAS Tribes get first go at promulgating.20  
This means it is States and TAS Tribes—not EPA—who get to make the policy calls.  If States’ and 
TAS Tribe’s standards are scientifically defensible, EPA approves.21  In its “secondary”22 and 
“backstop”23 role, EPA’s job is limited to reviewing the science underlying the State’s or TAS Tribe’s 
standards and to publishing guidance reflecting the latest scientific knowledge for states to consult.24  
  
But EPA has flipped the script.  In drafting this rule, EPA did not: 
 
- 
Alert the State25 that it was promulgating this rule; 
- 
Meet with the State; 
- 
Request input from the State; 
- 
Allow TAS Tribes to take first go at promulgating standards; or 
- 
Attempt to identify how this rule will apply in the State.

imposed these requirements to avoid creating future liabilities from unmaintained or poorly maintained facilities: the 
Safe Drinking Water Act expressly prohibits the provision of federal funding for water systems that do not meet capacity

permits, “EPA could use its oversight authority of approved programs [like APDES], which includes 
the authority to review permits.”26  This not-so-subtle threat means that EPA will review—and

Raise the whip, in other words, and make States submit.  EPA’s modus operandi.

Act (“ANCSA”) abolished all reservations in Alaska, excluding one.28  This has been recognized by 
the United States Supreme Court,29 Circuit Courts,30 and EPA itself.31  If EPA disagrees with this

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405-0245 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405 ---
August 3, 2023 
 
Attn: Docket ID No. EPA–HQ–OW–2016–0405 
U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 
Office of Water (Mail Code 4301T) 
1200 Pennsylvania Ave, NW 
Washington, DC 20460 
 
Submitted via: federalregister.gov 
 
Re: Comments on the Proposed Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations, EPA-HQ-
OW-2016-0405 
 
To the Office of Water, 
 
The Kansas Department of Health and Environment, Bureau of Water (KDHE) appreciates the opportunity to 
comment on the Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations, EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405 
(herein referred to as the Baseline WQS). 
 
Comments on the Proposed Baseline WQS: 
 
KDHE thanks EPA for the opportunity to not only provide comment on this proposed rule change but the 
multiple listening sessions held in addition to public hearings EPA conducted in support of this rulemaking. As 
was expressed verbally during multiple of these meetings, KDHE would like to formally reiterate some focused 
points of concern for the state in addition to showing support for the Association of Clean Water Administrators 
(ACWA) comment letter comprised of copious note-taking and review on the part of ACWA. 
 
KDHE agrees that surface waters should have Water Quality Standards (WQS), just as the Clean Water 
Act (CWA) intended. Waters lacking these Standards pose risk to adjacent lands and its users in addition to 
those waters downstream and all uses applicable to them as well. KDHE initially did not wish to take a position 
on the EPA pursuit of Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations as KDHE considered these 
standards to be beyond the authority and jurisdiction of KDHE. However, EPA’s proposal of translation from 
narrative to numeric criteria as currently presented has potentially massive ramifications for the state of Kansas. 
To reiterate, KDHE is not interested in taking a stance related to Tribal and EPA interactions as it is considered 
beyond our purview at this time; however, as the state of Kansas is impacted by this rule, KDHE provides these 

 
 
comments to provide perspective and suggestions for a more tenable and workable final rule in the pursuit of a 
more implementable approach to establishing these WQS. 
 
EPA has proposed adoption of narrative criteria as a starting point for the baseline WQS. Following the 50-
year history of the CWA, states similarly started with these criteria and have further expanded into the numeric 
criteria realm. KDHE considers a narrative criteria approach as a starting point to be in accordance with how 
WQS have developed over the life of the CWA and appropriate for this rulemaking. 
KDHE instead holds concern towards EPA’s proposed translations of narrative criteria to numeric criteria. 
The application of baseline WQS to tribal lands is, on the surface, an issue that would not be of impact to the 
state of Kansas as it pertains to WQS being applied to just those tribal lands without WQS already. However, 
the CWA requires protection of not just the water quality adjacent to or intersecting these lands but protection 
of the WQS applicable downstream as well. On EPA’s presentations of potentially affected tribal lands, the 4 of 
Kansas were denoted as possibly affected by the rule and the entire state of Oklahoma was excluded due to 
uncertainty from various legal rulings. Thus, the impacts of considering downstream protections are of direct 
interest to the state of Kansas and KDHE in its implementation of multiple CWA programs. 
EPA has presented 5 Options for translation of the narrative criteria to numeric (paraphrased from 
presentations): 
1. Translate the baseline narrative criteria using Section 304(a) criteria recommendations 
2. Translate the baseline narrative criteria using Section 304(a) criteria modified to reflect site-specific 
conditions 
3. Translate the baseline narrative criteria using numeric criteria available in Tribal WQS not yet CWA 
effective or adjacent state/tribal CWA-effective WQS 
4. Translate the baseline narrative criteria using required criteria methodologies for the Great Lakes Basin 
5. EPA may rely on existing CWA implementation provisions to translate applicable narrative criteria, as 
necessary 
Option 4 is not applicable to KDHE’s concerns as none of the waters west of the Mississippi River (all of 
Kansas) are upstream of the Great Lakes. Instead, KDHE’s concerns can be ranked in likelihood of the options 
expected to be chosen by EPA. In order, Options 1, 2, 5, and 3 give EPA extraordinary latitude for derivation of 
numeric criteria based on the narrative criteria. 
EPA has placed the authority and responsibility of execution of this rule on the EPA regional offices and 
staff. Considering no additional resources are expected to be provided to the regional offices for implementation 
of this rule, it is KDHE’s expectation that Options 1 & 2 will be the primary, if not sole, Options selected. This 
is further supported by the Economic Analysis for Proposed Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for 
Indian Reservations (EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405-0208 but titled “Baseline WQS NPRM Economic Analysis” on 
the docket; herein referred to as ‘Impact Analysis’) conducted by EPA where Option 1 was combined with 
Option 2 for their analysis. It is KDHE’s opinion that these options are the most likely to survive litigious action 
with minimal effort needed by EPA and will become the common practice of the regional offices in 
implementation of the baseline WQS as proposed. 
The majority of 304(a) criteria are of relatively minor concern to KDHE as presented in the current 
proposed rule as their adoption has been largely done in Kansas’ WQS already, along with subsequent 
implementation. However, select criteria and their protection if applied downstream are of potentially 
extraordinary consequence and burden to both the EPA regional offices but all entities upstream of where these 
baseline standards will be applied. 

 
 
 
 
Of particular concern among the 304(a) criteria are numeric nutrient criteria, both for lakes/reservoirs and 
the ecoregional approach for streams. Nutrient pollution is an ubiquitous concern nationwide and has been 
tackled in a nigh uncountable assortment of manners with multitudes of considerations made. Compared to 
almost every other pollutant among the 304(a) criteria with a concentration consideration and focus, how far to 
consider downstream uses applicable for nutrients could logically be declared as having no limit due to the 
loading being the primary concern as opposed to concentration in the water column. Thus, it is KDHE’s 
expectation that EPA will apply encompassing the entire watershed above where baseline WQS are applied 
with numeric nutrient criteria. 
The likelihood of EPA applying these criteria to the furthest upstream reaches of the watersheds may be 
reasonable speculation, and it has been observed EPA’s presumption of 5 miles upstream in their Impact 
Analysis. EPA has explicitly stated in meetings about these criteria that 5 miles isn’t the furthest upstream to 
consider for protection of downstream criteria, but it was used to get a reasonable estimate of the impact of the 
rule. However, there have been multiple examples KDHE can cite of far greater reaches than that consideration 
was forced to be had. Of most recent memory and as an example, a metals TMDL from Missouri had to 
consider Oklahoma’s WQS after having gone through 15 miles of Kansas before reaching Oklahoma. EPA has 
stated that the Impact Analysis is a good representation of what to expect from the rule. KDHE disagrees with 
this assessment of the impact estimates being reasonable based on the 5 mile approach alone from our previous 
experience with having to protect downstream water quality as required in the CWA. Instead, KDHE expects 
the full impact analysis of this rule could easily be orders of magnitude greater than EPA has currently 
accounted for. 
The expectation of impact of this rule being orders of magnitude greater than EPA is considered reasonable 
by KDHE due to how EPA conducted the Impact Analysis for the 5 miles upstream approach. Specifically, 
“EPA did not derive economic analysis criteria for nutrients.” The rationale provided is that values for TP and 
TN applied nationwide is not appropriate. Considering the phrasing didn’t suggest that TP or TN would be 
excluded from translation in the rule as presented but instead that it would be too complicated to assess the 
impacts, it is rational to presume EPA anticipates the translation of narrative criteria to numeric nutrient values. 
If the furthest upstream extent of the watershed is applied for a loading perspective of nutrient pollution, then 
the 5 miles upstream extent is entirely meaningless and grossly under-represented by EPA even if they had 
considered the impact of numeric nutrient criteria like should have been done for a reasonable impact analysis 
of this rule as proposed. The scope of this nutrient criteria translation would readily invalidate the $15-30 
million figures provided as expected implementation costs. 
The ambiguity of the rule holds particularly prescient for Oklahoma to our South, but due to the upstream 
reaches requiring protection of downstream uses also presents a major portion of Kansas as potentially being 
upstream of tribal baseline standards. Ambiguity is the only term we can use to define the potential scope of 
this, as EPA has not accounted for the tribal lands that would have baseline standards applied in Oklahoma. 
KDHE is sympathetic to the difficulties around the numerous legal challenges and rulings related to tribal lands 
in Oklahoma in recent years. However, the rule as presented does not preclude EPA’s responsibility of applying 
and interpreting these baseline standards. Without knowing even which lands the WQS will be applied to, how 
could it be considered rational to estimate the potential impact of the rule as presented? KDHE supports the 
difficulties expressed by Oklahoma in the meetings for trying to get EPA to provide their expectation of which 
lands will or will not have these baseline WQS applied. 

 
 
In summary, KDHE posits that EPA has not considered the full ramifications of this rule as proposed. The 
actual costs will likely be magnitudes greater than currently estimated, the regional staff will have extra WQS 
duties in addition to figuring out where the applicable tribal lands lie along with upstream protections for all 
NPDES permits both adjacent to but also upstream of these standards, and cross-jurisdictional management 
between states, tribes, and the multiple regional offices has not been fleshed out. This rule as presented holds 
significant potential towards crippling much of the CWA programs nationwide while things are worked out 
depending on the extents EPA applies this rule left to their discretion. 
 
KDHE has provided feedback on various tribal related issues in recent years as it concerns the CWA. Tribal 
Reserved Rights is of recent memory, and KDHE has been made aware of a particular distressing example 
related to Tribe as a State (TAS) authority in Iowa in the past two years. The tribe gained TAS authority but did 
not adopt WQS. EPA Region 7 proceeded to adopt WQS as required under the CWA. In the process of so 
doing, EPA published the proposed standards in the Des Moines Register. Subsequently, the state of Iowa was 
surprised to find numeric nutrient criteria applicable to many of their NPDES permitted dischargers upstream of 
this tribal land. Upon further review of this scenario, there are two factors KDHE wishes to bring to EPA’s 
attention; the first as it relates to this rule, and the second as a call for standardization of public notification 
where it is currently lacking. 
The first consideration as it relates to this rule is that EPA is not holding itself to the same CWA obligations 
as states with a public hearing required for revision of the WQS along with a triennial review frequency. This 
has been observed in other comment letters, and KDHE is in support of EPA being held to the same 
requirements as currently operating WQS programs nationwide. Opening governmental actions, policies, and 
laws to public engagement is at the core of the CWA. KDHE recommends revision of the rule to allow and 
require for the availability of this public involvement and consideration in future revisions to these WQS. 
The second consideration stems from the publication of the aforementioned scenario in the Des Moines 
register. For states that have garnered various CWA authorities for WQS, TMDLs, etc., the state register is the 
commonly accepted, or usually specified by state law such as the case with Kansas, location for revisions to 
these programs to be presented. Notably, this restriction is not placed in the CFRs currently nor in the CWA for 
where tribal public hearings shall be published for the public to attend and provide comments nor any other 
entity in which the EPA is the designated authority of CWA programs. It is with KDHE’s strong urging that 
EPA consider this procedural loophole that has already presented surprise and confusion in Iowa before it 
contributes to further problems nationwide and has the potential for intentional obfuscation as well. As other 
states have mentioned on calls with EPA about this rulemaking but for different reasons, a standardized means 
of following revisions to baseline WQS, TAS changes, etc. should be done so it is clear and effective for all 
interested parties to follow these revisions. Government should not be run with surprise but through clear, 
openly communicated, and publicly engaged actions. 
 
KDHE recommends further consideration of the implementation impacts of this rule prior to final adoption 
by EPA. It is common practice with EPA to adopt numeric criteria and subsequently release technical support 
materials a year or two later. For states running their WQS programs, it is now common practice to defer 
adoption of the new criteria until these implementation procedures are made available and able to be discerned. 
The rule as proposed doesn’t have the option of adopting these criteria or not and waiting for implementation to 
be figured out before doing so. The affected tribes will have 90 days to defer or else baseline WQS are the law 
of the land, with no consideration for implementation being realistically worked out as currently proposed. The 

 
 
ramifications for tribal lands are beyond KDHE’s scope, but for what could be applied upstream to the state of 
Kansas, as discussed above, may be immeasurable but certainly hasn’t been measured reasonably by EPA yet. 
In order to meet EPA’s current target of formal adoption next year, KDHE proposes two potential solutions 
to simplify most of our concerns for baseline WQS specifically. One proposed solution KDHE has observed in 
other comments and discussions is defaulting to Option 3 by using a neighboring state’s WQS or tribal WQS in 
the works. The second proposed solution KDHE would like to present is instead of all 304(a) criteria, EPA start 
with the priority pollutants. The sheer ambiguity and potential scope of all 304(a) criteria is beyond what has 
been considered by EPA, but it is KDHE’s opinion that the desired improvements in management of water 
quality on lands without WQS would see significant jumps with the priority pollutants specifically as they are 
far more implementable and almost ubiquitously in practice in all states’ WQS currently. The CWA was put in 
place 50 years ago and much of the WQS progress during that time has been done in increments; simply 
skipping all 50 years can be considered ambitious but also reckless if not fully considered in scale. 
 
Thank you for the opportunity to provide comment on the Baseline WQS. If you have any questions regarding 
these comments, or would like to discuss any of them, please feel free to contact me at (785) 296-5508 or 
Dane.Boring@ks.gov.  
 
Sincerely, 
 
Dane Boring 
Policy Planning & Standards Unit Chief 
Kansas Department of Health & Environment – Bureau of Water

Subject: Comments Docket ID No. EPA–HQ–OW–2016–0405, Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards 
for Indian Reservations 
 
Dear Director Hisel-McCoy: 
 
Thank you for the opportunity to review and provide comments to the proposed rule for Federal 
Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations, dated May 5, 2023.  This proposed rule is very 
comprehensive and will have an enormous economic impact on the citizens of Idaho and the Idaho 
Department of Environmental Quality (IDEQ).  Although IDEQ agrees with the intent of the rule and that 
waters in our beautiful state lacking water quality standards pose a risk to all immediate and 
downstream waters users, however this proposed rule is flawed in its implementation and IDEQ does 
not feel that EPA adequately engaged with IDEQ or our stakeholders during the development.  IDEQ 
does not support the establishment of federal baseline water quality standards for Indian reservation 
waters.  Below we highlight a few of our main concerns. 
I. 
 EPA’s definition of reservation waters.   
Under the proposed rule, baseline water quality standards would apply to waters “within the borders of 
an Indian reservation” but goes on to state that “in accordance with judicial precedent, the term 
“reservation” includes both formal reservation and informal reservation such as trust land that has been 
validly set apart for use by the tribe even if such trust land is located outside the exterior boundaries of 
a formally designated reservation”1.  In the state of Idaho, we have five Tribes in which several have 
trust lands outside of reservations.   
In the Association of Clean Water Administrators (ACWA)/EPA listening session IDEQ participated in, EPA 
clearly admits that they are unclear as to the boundaries or lands that will be covered under this rule 
and each situation will be unique and will need to be “sorted out” on a permit-by-permit basis.  This lack 
of certainty adds to the time and expense of moving forward with regulatory decisions.  Highlighting the 
lack of clarity on trust lands, EPA went on to say that it does not determine reservation boundaries, and 
that “the responsibility lies elsewhere in the government.” However, it appears that even between 
agencies and states, data is inconsistent and has yet to be centralized. For example, data provided by 
 
1 56 FR 64876, 64881, December 12, 1991 

August 2023 
 
Page 2 of 4 
 
the Bureau of Indian Affairs in response to a 2018 Freedom of Information Request submitted by the 
Native Lands Advocacy Project, shows that Idaho has 1,057,430 acres of trust land,2 not including fee or 
restricted lands.  It took BIA nearly two years to fulfill this request for data, and there were hundreds of 
thousands of discrepancies between the area of land reported by BIA, and that recorded by the States.3 
In the preamble of the proposed rule, it states that EPA has the authority to treat an Indian tribe as a 
state (TAS) pertaining to water resources within the borders of an Indian reservation.  Clean Water Act 
(CWA) section 5184  defines TAS as all land within the limits of any Indian reservation under the 
jurisdiction of the United States Government, notwithstanding the issuance of any patent, and including 
rights-of-way running through the reservation.  EPA states that, under this language, tribes can only 
obtain TAS status over waters within the borders of their reservations, and conversely, tribes cannot 
obtain TAS under the CWA for water resources pertaining to any non-reservation Indian country.  
However, in this proposed rule, EPA then states that Indian reservation includes trust lands validly set 
aside for Indian tribes even if such lands have not formally been designated as an Indian reservation.  
These two statements appear to be, at least in part, inconsistent.  On the one hand, EPA states that TAS 
status will only apply to waters within the borders of a reservation, but on the other hand, appears to 
state that waters on trust lands, even if outside reservation borders may be included within the 
definition of water resources of an Indian reservation. 
IDEQ questions whether EPA is correct in its assertion that the revised interpretation will have no effect 
on existing state CWA programs and requests clarification on whether States will be preempted from 
applying State water quality law.  Under EPA’s current interpretation, there is no express delegation of 
authority to tribes.  Thus, prior to EPA approving TAS status for a tribe, tribal authority is limited to the 
tribes’ inherent authority to regulate non-tribal members.  With certain exceptions, tribes cannot 
regulate non-tribal members on non-tribal land within an Indian reservation.  States, on the other hand, 
generally have the authority to regulate non-tribal members on such land.  In addition, even when a 
tribe has the inherent authority to regulate non-tribal members on non-tribal land, States may have 
concurrent jurisdiction to apply state water quality law.    
Under EPA’s revised interpretation, section 518(e)5 provides an express delegation to Indian tribes to 
regulate all water resources within the borders of a reservation, even those waters on non-tribal 
member lands.  Under EPA’s revised interpretation, IDEQ will be preempted from applying state water 
quality law to non-members on non-tribal land, even prior to the approval of TAS status for a tribe.  If 
this is true, then EPA’s revised interpretation will certainly have an impact on existing state CWA 
programs.  IDEQ requests EPA clarify whether under the revised interpretation, States will be preempted 
from applying state water quality law to non-tribal members on non-tribal land within the borders of a 
reservation and if there is a preemption, whether the state is preempted prior to EPA approval of TAS 
status.   
 
 
 
 
2 https://data.nativeland.info/dataset/2019-tribal-land-ownership-by-state 
3Brewer, J. P., Carroll, S. R., Bartecchi, D., Chesnais, A. K., & Johnson, M. K. (2023). Life and times of data access: 
Regarding Native Lands. Environment and Planning F, 2(1–2), 305–315. 
https://doi.org/10.1177/26349825231164616

II. 
EPA’s role and responsibilities and implementation considerations. 
This section provides DEQ’s comments on EPA’s proposed rule implementation and lack of concrete 
language on which to provide meaningful comment. EPA did not adequately incorporate stakeholder 
involvement and input when developing tribal baseline water quality standards. Without allowing for 
upstream stakeholder engagement early in the process of permit development, the new tribal standards 
may lead to unnecessary conflict between upstream discharge permits and new standards.  Under the 
CWA, states have been increasingly required to provide more rule development and water quality 
standards implementation transparency, and public and stakeholder engagement throughout all 
programs.  However, this proposed rule does not require the same transparency from EPA.  EPA has not 
identified how TMDL’s, water quality assessments and listings, or other CWA programs will be 
developed on reservation waters and implemented or how the tribes and state will be part of the 
process.  
The intent of the “binding translation procedures” is unclear.  EPA “is relying on its role as both the 
promulgating entity and the primary implementation authority to allow a degree of site-specific tailoring 
with the regulatory construct of the water quality standards during subsequent implementation of the 
proposed rule.”  In other words, EPA will only develop the water quality criteria component of standards 
when implementing a TMDL, NDPES permit, or water quality certification. A situation which would never 
be approved in a state’s WQS package.  
Narrative criteria will be accompanied by a binding translation procedure for determining case-specific 
numeric values to protect the applicable designated use.  Again, the translation procedures are vague, 
and it is unclear how the process will be implemented for the development of NPDES permits limits, 
water quality assessment, or water quality certification development. States are unable to provide 
adequate feedback for implementation if there isn’t a clear process or procedure provided to comment 
on. During the ACWA/EPA listing session, EPA clarified that the first time a state would be able to review 
the binding translation and the resulting criteria would be when the NPDES permit is out for review, 
which is very late in the development process.  
Furthermore, EPA states it would maintain a website containing a publicly available list of all tribes with 
Indian reservations for purposes of this proposed rule.  It would also indicate which of those tribes are 
covered by baseline WQS, which are excluded from coverage, and which tribes already have CWA-
effective WQS in place. Updates and changes to permits will also be housed on this site. This is a large 
amount of information requiring frequent updates. This is not an effective way to inform states or 
stakeholders and would be difficult to maintain.  
During this same listening session, EPA clarified that they will not be reviewing the narrative standards 
or the translated criteria under a triennial review.  The CWA requires states and authorized tribes to 
periodically review and, as appropriate, adopt new or revised WQS to meet the requirements of the 
CWA6.  This appears to hold states to a higher regulatory burden than EPA is willing or able to 
implement. 
EPA “is not proposing to define cultural and traditional uses in more detail in this rule…”. This is another 
example of ambiguity that is nearly impossible for a state or stakeholder to provide meaningful 
feedback on. It is unclear how, by whom, or where the cultural and traditional use will be defined and 
implemented.  The proposed rule,7 recognizes tribal reserved rights to use and access natural and 
 
6 Water Quality Standards Handbook, Chapter 6: Procedures for Review and Revision of Water Quality Standards. 
7 EPA docket# EPA-HQ-OW-2021-079 

August 2023 
 
Page 4 of 4 
 
cultural resources but does not identify how it will identify and protect those tribal cultural and 
traditional uses and tribal reserved rights.  
IDEQ understands that EPA was deliberately vague as to how to designate and implement the protection 
of Outstanding Natural Resource Waters (ONRW).  IDEQ has a clear and transparent process as to how 
an ONRW is nominated and designated.  Since the ONRW would only apply within reservation 
boundaries for this rule, it is imperative that the process is clearly laid out so IDEQ can have a more 
predictive process as an upstream state. 
III. 
Economic Impact 
Idaho has many permitted dischargers upstream from reservation and trust lands.  The economic 
analysis that EPA conducted only included facilities with in a five-mile radius of reservation lands.  This is 
dramatically underestimated.  Much depends on the regulated constituent, and Idaho has many 
naturally occurring metals persistent in the watershed because of regional geology. Consequently, these 
will be detectable further than five miles.  For example, EPA is currently writing a TMDL for the Spokane 
River that crosses from Idaho to Washington state.  The Spokane reservation is 58 river miles from the 
Idaho border. Idaho will likely have to meet a much more restrictive PCB criteria than our own state’s 
approved standard and more stringent than even Washington state’s PCB criteria. EPA contends that for 
Washington State to meet the criteria at the border of the reservation, then too shall Idaho have to 
meet a more stringent criteria than state standards.  This will cause substantial expense both internally 
to IDEQ with updated permit development and review, and the permittees to adjust their treatment to 
accommodate stricter limits.  In addition, when stricter limits are required on an upstream permit, often 
there are not sufficiently sensitive test methods in place for monitoring some pollutants, again creating 
opportunities for compliance legal issues.   
In closing, during the meeting on July 13, 2023, hosted by ACWA, EPA stated that there will not be any 
additional funding for implementation of this rule.  This proposed rule will have a tremendous amount 
of additional work for EPA staff, who are already often behind schedule for current CWA program 
response.  IDEQ has concerns that the additional work this rule will entail, EPA staff will not have the 
ability to respond in a timely manner, negatively affecting Idaho citizens and stakeholders.

Mary Anne Nelson 
Surface & Wastewater Division Administrator

Beth Spelsberg, IDEQ

--- COMMENT ID: EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405-0238 AND DOCKET ID: EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405 ---
August 3, 2023 
 
Michael S. Regan 
United States Environmental Protection Agency 
EPA Docket Center, Office of Water Docket 
1200 Pennsylvania Avenue NW 
Washington, DC 20460 
 
RE: Docket ID No. EPA-HQ-OW-2016-0405, Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for 
Indian Reservations.  
 
Administrator Regan, 
 
The United States Environmental Protection Agency’s (“EPA”) Federal Baseline Water Quality 
Standards for Indian Reservations, (the “proposed rule”) presents novel and unique requirements 
which will press States into a quagmire of flawed regulatory burdens. These burdens will fall 
particularly hard on Idaho, whose boundaries include five federally recognized Indian tribes. Not 
only is the proposed rule bad policy, but it is also unlawful and exceeds statutory authority. Here 
again, EPA illegitimately asserts federal power with no statutory basis. The Clean Water Act 
(“CWA”) contains no language authorizing EPA’s astonishing power grab. Idaho herein comments 
on the important issues arising from EPA’s proposed action.1 
 
DISCUSSION 
 
I. 
Background 
 
EPA seeks to establish water quality standards (“WQS”) for waters on Indian reservations currently 
without EPA-approved WQS under the CWA. These WQS (“baseline WQS”) are intended to 
establish human health and environmental objectives as the basis for CWA protections. EPA 
intends for baseline WQS to apply to all waters of the United States in “Indian country.” EPA 
defines “Indian country” the same as 18 U.S.C. 1151,2 but provides automatic exclusions. In all, 
 
1 The first indication of EPA’s lack of respect for the role of the States—and contrary to language of the CWA—
throughout the proposed rule EPA capitalizes “tribe” and uses lower case for “states”. Apparently, this is an attempt 
to elevate tribal status above that of States by implication, in direct conflict with CWA’s treatment of those terms. 
 
2 (a) All land within the limits of any Indian reservation under the jurisdiction of the United States Government, 
notwithstanding the issuance of any patent, and, including rights-of-way running through the reservation; (b) all 
dependent Indian communities within the borders of the United States whether within the original or subsequently 
acquired territory thereof, and whether within or without the limits of a state; and (c) all Indian allotments, the Indian 
titles to which have not been extinguished, including rights-of-way running through the same 

2 of 9 
 
the proposed rule will apply to all waters of the United States within the limits of any Indian 
reservation under the jurisdiction of the United States, except on those reservations for which EPA 
or tribes have established WQS.  
 
The proposed rule would establish the following nationwide federal water quality standards for all 
waters of the United States within the limits of Indian reservations: (1) Designated uses consistent 
with the CWA including a new designated use that protects cultural and traditional uses; (2) 
narrative water quality criteria translated through binding EPA “options” to protect those new uses; 
(3) an antidegradation policy including requirements for the development of total maximum daily 
loads (“TMDLs”) and requirements for recognizing and revising impaired waters status; and, (4) 
a mixing zone policy and compliance schedule authorizing provision. See 88 Fed. Reg. 29496-01, 
129499, Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations. 
 
The proposed rule will have an enormous and negative economic impact on the citizens of Idaho 
and its agencies. The proposed rule is flawed, and the State of Idaho does not support the 
establishment of federal baseline water quality standards for waters on Indian reservations.   
 
II. 
The EPA lacks authority to adopt the proposed rule. 
 
EPA seeks to broadly promulgate baseline WQS for all waters on Indian reservations with no 
statutory authority to do so. EPA asserts that its authority for the proposed rule is supplied by CWA 
section 303(c)(4)(B). Specifically, the preamble to the proposed rule states that on January 18, 
2001,3  EPA administrator Carol Browner made a finding, under CWA section 303(c)(4)(B), that 
“water quality standards are necessary to meet the requirements of the Clean Water Act for all 
Indian country waters” without established WQS.  
 
CWA section 303(c)(4)(B) allows EPA to “promptly prepare and publish proposed regulations 
setting forth a revised or new water quality standard for the navigable waters. . . where the 
Administrator determines that a revised or new standard is necessary.” As more fully set forth 
below, EPA’s 2001 necessity determination is inadequate to justify the broad promulgation of WQS 
on all Indian reservations. See 40 C.F.R. § 131.22. Finally, EPA’s creation of a new designated use 
for cultural and traditional protection lacks statutory authority.  
 
A. Promulgating and implementing WQS for tribal waters exceeds EPA’s statutory 
authority. 
 
In 1987, Congress added Section 518 to the CWA, making tribes eligible for treatment as states 
(“TAS”) under the CWA. See 33 U.S.C. § 1377. Once authorized as a TAS, tribes would be granted 
regulatory power to, inter alia, establish water quality standards and certify compliance for federal 
license and permit holders, NPDES permitting, and CWA section 404 permitting. The proposed 
rule exceeds EPA’s CWA jurisdiction, because EPA seeks to promulgate tribal-specific WQS for 
waters on reservations even though those tribes have not obtained TAS authorization.  
 
3 This finding was included in a proposed rule, Federal Water Quality Standards for Indian Country and Other 
Provisions Regarding Federal Water Quality Standards, signed only two days before President George W. Bush was 
sworn in. The proposed rule was never published in the Federal Register, nor were public comments requested upon 
it. EPA’s claim that the proposal was withdrawn to “allow additional review” is dubious, at best.  

3 of 9 
 
 
Congress created a clear avenue for regulation of waters on reservations, CWA section 518, which 
contains specific directives to the EPA regarding the regulation of waters on reservations. The legal 
maxim, expressio unius est exclusio alterius, applies here, and the “EPA cannot rely on its general 
authority to make rules necessary to carry out its functions when a specific statutory directive 
defines the relevant functions of EPA in a particular area.”  Am. Petroleum Inst. v. U.S. E.P.A., 52 
F.3d 1113, 1119 (D.C. Cir. 1995).   
 
Section 518 describes three statutory directives to set tribal WQS—none of which enable EPA to 
jump the line and regulate waters on reservations. First, a tribe may negotiate a cooperative 
agreement with an adjoining State to apply that State’s standards. 33 U.S.C. § 1377(d); 56 Fed. 
Reg. at 64889. Second, the tribe may adopt the adjoining state’s standards. 56 Fed. Reg. at 64889. 
Finally, a tribe may adopt standards independently. Id.  
 
EPA now attempts to promulgate and implement WQS, in excess of its CWA jurisdiction, despite 
the clear grant of authority to the tribes to do so in Section 518. See Michigan v. E.P.A., 268 F.3d 
1075, 1082-87 (D.C. Cir. 2001) (EPA lacked general federal authority and jurisdiction in disputed 
Indian country because EPA's only authority to act for tribes under Clean Air Act (“CAA”) first 
required a finding of tribal jurisdiction based on TAS status under the CAA); see also 54 Fed. Reg. 
at 39103 (“EPA emphasizes that Federal promulgation is not consistent with the intent of CWA 
sections 303(c) and 518(e) to provide States, and Tribes qualifying for treatment as States, with 
the first opportunity to set standards)”. 
 
Moreover, EPA has not given any explanation for reversing its previous position that State WQS 
should be the logical starting point for reservation WQS. In promulgating “Amendments to the 
Water Quality Standards Regulation That Pertain to Standards on Indian Reservations,” EPA 
“assume[d] that existing State standards are applicable to reservation waters.” 56 Fed. Reg. at 
64891 (citing 54 Fed. Reg. 39104). In relation to federal promulgation of tribal WQS, “EPA re-
assert[ed] its belief that the standards of the adjacent State will be a logical beginning step for EPA 
if for no other reason than the consistency required by 40 CFR 131.10(b),” and clarified that 
Federal promulgation of standards occurs only as “a last resort”—that is, when “it finds a particular 
need” Id.  
 
The proposed rule, however, finds no particularized need justifying its broad-sweeping proposed 
action to treat all waters on reservations the same without regard for the unique topography, 
hydrology, geology, climate, and the resulting rivers, streams, and lakes that make up the water 
bodies within individual reservations. The power to promulgate a particular standard, one case at 
a time, does not encompass the power to promulgate every type of WQS in innumerable cases, 
nationwide, over an EPA-estimated 76,000 miles of rivers and streams and 1.9 million acres of 
lakes, reservoirs, and other open surface waters. In the absence of finding a “particular need” to 
set reservation WQS, EPA unjustifiably seizes authority for WQS promulgation from the tribes 
and States. 
 
 

4 of 9 
 
B. EPA’s creation of a new cultural designated use exceeds its CWA authority. 
 
Identifying designated uses is the first step in establishing WQS, and the proposed rule creates a 
new designated use to protect cultural and traditional uses of on-reservation waters. The CWA 
grants States (and authorized tribes) authority to determine designated uses—EPA has no CWA 
authority to create new designated uses.  
 
The Act prescribes that WQS, which include designated uses and water quality criteria to protect 
those uses, “shall be such as to protect the public health or welfare, enhance the quality of water 
and serve the purposes of this chapter,” and “shall be established taking into consideration their 
use and value for public water supplies, propagation of fish and wildlife, recreational purposes, 
and agricultural, industrial, and other purposes, and also taking into consideration their use and 
value for navigation.” 33 U.S.C. § 1313(c)(2)(A). If adopting new or revised designated uses other 
than the uses specified in CWA section 101(a)(2),4 or removing designated uses, States must 
submit documentation justifying how their consideration of the use and value of water for those 
uses appropriately supports the State's action. See 40 C.F.R. § 131.10. 
 
Offering guidance on CWA section 101(a)(2), EPA has identified uses of waters that are considered 
desirable to maintain water quality for the protection and propagation of fish, shellfish, wildlife, 
and recreation water when considering the use and value of State waters. These uses5 align with 
the CWA. While States are free to develop and adopt any use classification system they see as 
appropriate, see 40 CFR 131.10(a), no such authority exists for the EPA to create new designated 
uses without clear textual authority in the CWA6 and none exists.  
 
EPA states: “CWA section 303(c)(2)(A) provides that uses are to protect the “public health or 
welfare”” and because “[c]ultural and traditional uses serve to protect the health and welfare of 
Tribal members exercising such uses,” that such uses are “within the purposes enumerated in the 
Act.” 88 Fed. Reg. at 29503. Under the Act, WQS are intended to protect, inter alia, public health 
and welfare “taking into consideration their use and value for public water supplies, propagation 
of fish and wildlife, recreational purposes, and agricultural, industrial, and other purposes, and also 
taking into consideration their use and value for navigation.” 33 U.S.C. §1313 (c)(2)(A) (emphasis 
added). In the proposed rule, EPA works backwards from the statutory scheme. Instead of CWA’s 
process of identifying a designated use and then setting standards to protect it, EPA starts with the 
goal of protecting tribal culture and fabricates a designated use based on policy absent from the 
 
4 “[T]he protection and propagation of fish, shellfish, and wildlife and provides for recreation in and on the water.” 
 
5 See EPA Water Quality Standards Handbook at 2, EPA-823-B-12-002 (2012) (public water supplies, propagation of 
fish and wildlife, recreation, agriculture and industrial purposes, and navigation: public water supplies; protection and 
propagation of fish, shellfish, and wildlife; recreation; agriculture; industry; navigation; coral reef preservation; 
marinas; groundwater recharge; aquifer protection; and, hydroelectric power). 
 
6 EPA has stated that any “Federal proposed rulemaking would likely be very straightforward, all streams would be 
classified fishable/swimmable and the criteria to protect the uses would be those guidance values established by EPA 
under section 304(a) of the Act. Any changes in a final rule would depend on information submitted during the public 
comment period.” 56 Fed. Reg. at 64891. Yet, by the proposed rule, EPA has neglected the straightforward approach 
of adopting existing classifications—opting instead for the creation of a new, statutorily unauthorized designated use 
classification.  
 

5 of 9 
 
CWA. Cultural and traditional use is simply not an enumerated purpose of the CWA, and EPA 
cannot make it so by agency fiat. 
 
EPA relies on CWA section 303(c)(2)(A) as authority for EPA setting new designated uses. Section 
303(c)(2)(A) provides that “[w]henever the State revises or adopts a new standard... [those] 
standards shall be such as to protect the public health or welfare, enhance the quality of water and 
serve the purposes of this chapter.” (emphasis added). Section 303(c)(2)(A) provides authority to 
the States (or TAS) to create new standards consisting of designated uses and water quality criteria 
to protect those uses—it is not a grant of authority to the EPA to create designated uses binding on 
States and tribes. Thus, even if cultural and traditional use is a proper “other purpose” or use under 
CWA section 303(c)(2)(A), only the States are empowered to establish that designated use, not the 
EPA. 
 
Finally, EPA fails to explain why a “cultural and traditional use” serves to protect the health and 
welfare of tribal members. EPA fails to justify creating a new designated use, when the existing 
uses of public water supplies, protection and propagation of fish, shellfish, and wildlife, or 
recreation currently protect public health and welfare and are intended to do so. Federal creation 
of a designated use divorced from the text of the CWA lacks any statutory authority.7 
 
The CWA does not prescribe precisely what designated uses a State or TAS must select, it merely 
grants the right to do so, while considering public water supplies, propagation of fish and wildlife, 
and recreational, agricultural, industrial, and navigational purposes. The proposed rule, without 
any statutory authority to do so, commandeers that right, precluding the discretion the CWA grants 
to Idaho. 
 
C. EPA is not authorized to require ESA protections. 
 
The proposed rule’s narrative criteria requires that “[A]ll waters shall be free from conditions that 
would likely jeopardize the continued existence of any threatened or endangered species listed 
under the Federal Endangered Species Act or result in the destruction or adverse modification of 
such species' critical habitat.” 88 Fed. Reg. at 29505. The CWA does not provide EPA authority to 
mandate the protection of endangered species under the Endangered Species Act. To the extent 
EPA is required to consult pursuant to section 7(a)(2) of the Endangered Species Act, EPA has not 
done so yet—nor has EPA provided a mechanism for such consultation as a part of setting baseline 
WQS or amending that baseline.  
 
III. 
There are no regulatory gaps in WQS on reservations. 
 
EPA asserts a Baseline WQS Rule is required because there is a regulatory absence of WQS in 
effect on Indian reservations under the CWA. See 88 FR at 29499 (“the majority of Indian 
reservations do not have this foundational protection laid out by Congress in the CWA for their 
waters”). EPA contends that “[t]he lack of CWA-effective WQS for most Indian reservations means 
that those waters do not have the human health and environmental objectives in place that form 
 
7 EPA’s concomitant efforts to force States to identify, create, and protect tribal designated uses, see 87 Fed. Reg. 
74361-01, is a similar attempt to usurp Congressional authority and rewrite the purpose and scope of the CWA. 

6 of 9 
 
the basis for CWA protections,” and that “promulgation of baseline WQS would safeguard water 
quality until Tribes obtain TAS and adopt CWA WQS themselves.” Id.  
 
EPA wrongly concludes that no WQS apply on Indian reservations for CWA purposes. The CWA 
is a comprehensive nationwide environmental regulatory structure that does not omit waters on 
Indian reservations from the scope of State WQS protections. See 33 U.S.C. §§ 1251, 1313; see 
also 57 Fed. Reg. 60848, 60849 (Dec. 22, 1992) (EPA acknowledgment that CWA is designed to 
ensure that “all waters” are sufficiently clean); id. at 60851 (EPA's initial efforts under the 1972 
CWA aimed to expand coverage to “all interstate and intrastate surface waters”). 
 
The CWA requirement that States promulgate WQS for all intrastate waters is unambiguously 
comprehensive and necessarily includes all waters on Indian reservations within state boundaries. 
See Thomas v. Jackson, 581 F.3d 658, 661 (8th Cir. 2009) (CWA requires each State to establish 
WQS for waterbodies “within the state’s boundaries”); National Wildlife Federation v. Browner, 
127 F.3d 1126, 1127 (D.C. Cir. 1997) (CWA requires state WQS for “every” body of water within 
a State); Friends of Merrymeeting Bay v. Olsen, 839 F.Supp.2d 366,370 (D. Me. 2012) (CWA 
requires States to establish WQS for “all” of their waterbodies); see also 33 U.S.C. § 1370 (nothing 
in the CWA “shall. . . be construed as impairing or in any manner affecting any right or jurisdiction 
of the States with respect to the waters (including boundary waters) of such States”). 
 
The CWA’s plain requirement that States promulgate WQS for all intrastate waters, including 
waters within Indian reservations, is also supported by the Supreme Court’s decision in Federal 
Power Commission v. Tuscarora Indian Nation, 362 U.S. 99, 116 (1960) (“a general statute in 
terms applying to all persons includes Indians and their property interests”), as well as a subsequent 
Court of Appeals decision in the analogous water regulatory context of the Safe Drinking Water 
Act. Phillips Petroleum Co. v. U.S. E.P.A., 803 F.2d 545, 555-57 (10th Cir. 1986) (SDWA, which 
establishes a “national policy with respect to clean water,” applies to Indian lands consistent with 
Tuscarora rule and EPA’s contemporaneous interpretations of the SDWA; to hold otherwise would 
contradict the clear meaning and purpose of the SDWA by creating a “vacuum of authority” and 
“leaving vast areas of the nation devoid of protection”). 
 
EPA’s own WQS regulations and historical guidance on WQS also emphasize the role of States in 
comprehensively promulgating WQS for all intrastate waters. See 40 C.F.R. §§ 131.1, 131.2, 
131.3(j), 131.4, 131.8, 131.10; Questions and Answers on Water Quality Standards Under PL 92-
500, U.S. EPA, Office of Water, November 1972, question no. 4 (WQS apply to all interstate and 
intrastate waters), no. 6 (States must establish WQS for all intrastate waters); Questions & Answers 
on: Water Quality Standards, U.S. EPA, Office of Water, December 1983, question no. 2 (WQS 
are established by States), no. 9 (WQS apply to waters of the United States), no. 22 (all States have 
WQS)); Introduction To Water Quality Standards, U.S. EPA, Office of Water, September 1988, 
Section I, question no. 7 (WQS should be adopted for each waterbody within a State's boundaries), 
no. 8 (States adopt WQS), no. 10 (WQS apply to all waters of the United States); Water Quality 
Standards Handbook: Second Edition, with update # I, U.S. EPA, Office of Water, August 1994 
(select portions only), Introduction (1972 CWA amendments extended the previously established 
program of WQS for interstate waters to one requiring WQS for all intrastate and surface waters). 
 

7 of 9 
 
Finally, as discussed in section II.A supra, it has been EPA’s “belief that the standards of the 
adjacent State will be a logical beginning step for EPA if for no other reason than the consistency 
required by 40 CFR 131.10(b).” 56 Fed. Reg. at 64891. That is, “existing State standards are 
applicable to reservation waters,” id. (citing 54 Fed. Reg. 39104), no regulatory gap exists for 
Indian reservations, and, contrary to EPA’s assertion, those waters do indeed “have the human 
health and environmental objectives in place that form the basis for CWA protections,” by way of 
State promulgated WQS.  
 
IV. 
The proposed rule is vague and ambiguous. 
 
Uncertainty surrounds the legal boundaries of many tribal lands in the West. How will EPA 
determine the boundaries of reservations and trust lands, particularly where those boundaries are 
subject to ongoing dispute? Additionally, non-tribal ownership of lands within reservation 
boundaries are often subject to state rather than tribal laws. How will EPA develop and enforce 
WQS standards across lands that alternate between tribal and non-tribal ownership? In Idaho, there 
are significant outstanding issues regarding the borders of Indian reservations, as well as issues of 
land patents and rights-of-way running through reservations. Additionally, there are concerns 
about how the baseline WQS will impact Idaho’s ability to apply State water quality laws to non-
tribal members on tribal lands. The proposed rule fails to address these issues, leaving the extent 
of the rule’s applicability vague and ambiguous. 
 
Additionally, EPA’s proposed “cultural and traditional uses” are so tribe-specific that a national 
catch-all designation of “cultural and traditional uses” appears so vague as to have little meaning. 
Cultural and ceremonial differences between tribes and groups within tribes also can create 
challenges in their implementation. It is not clear how these uses would be defined in rule or 
implemented in practice. EPA does not even attempt to define cultural and traditional uses, 
recognizing the ambiguity of the term. The outcome of this will potentially be a very confusing 
and inconsistent administrative framework for state regulators, regulated entities, and impacted 
citizens. 
 
The proposed rule discusses the negative impacts of suppressed fish consumption rates, describing 
the “suppression effect” as when the “fish consumption rate for a given tribe reflects a current level 
of consumption that is artificially diminished relative to the tribe’s heritage fish consumption rate.” 
88 Fed. Reg. at 29521. EPA’s reliance on the undefined “suppression effect” creates additional 
problems. EPA’s hope to achieve heritage consumption rates through water quality standards 
ignores the myriad causes outside the scope of the CWA that have reduced fish consumption rates 
among tribes.8 
 
The intent of the “binding translation procedures” is unclear. EPA “is relying on its role as both the 
promulgating entity and the primary implementation authority to allow a degree of site-specific 
tailoring with the regulatory construct of the water quality standards during subsequent 
implementation of the proposed rule.” In other words, EPA will only develop the water quality 
 
8 EPA asserts that its proposed rule will “promote[] tribal sovereignty over tribes’ water resources.” 88 Fed. Reg. at 
29521. While tribes may apply for TAS status to regulate water quality, States and not tribes retain sovereignty control 
over water resources. See 33 U.S.C. § 1251(g).  

8 of 9 
 
criteria component of standards when implementing a TMDL, NDPES permit, or water quality 
certification. This would never be approved in a State’s WQS package. 
 
The proposed rule’s “narrative criteria” will be accompanied by a binding translation procedure 
for determining case-specific numeric values to protect the applicable designated use. The 
translation procedures are vague, and it is unclear how the process will be implemented for the 
development of NPDES permits limits, water quality assessment, or water quality certification 
development. 
 
The proposed rule’s narrative criteria state that “all waters shall maintain a level of water quality 
at the pour points to downstream waters that provide for the attainment and maintenance of the 
water quality standards.” 88 Fed. Reg. 29505 (emphasis added). EPA defines “pour points” as “the 
points of entry into downstream water bodies.” Id. This definition does not exist in the CWA, nor 
does the Act imply that EPA should legislate this amendment to the Act by agency edict.9  
Moreover, in Idaho, there are significant outstanding issues regarding the borders of Indian 
reservations, as well as issues of land patents and rights-of-way running through reservations. The 
proposed rule’s ill-defined “pour point” does nothing to address these concerns, and will only 
create regulatory confusion.  
 
Finally, the proposed rule’s narrative criteria recommends that in addition to site-specific 
conditions based on a sound scientific rationale, “indigenous knowledge” should be considered. 
88 Fed. Reg. at 29507. EPA relies on a November 30, 2022 internal memorandum as its basis for 
“indigenous knowledge”. This document is not an agency interpretation and has not undergone 
rulemaking—yet, EPA relies on it for general applicability. The CWA does not provide for the 
consideration of indigenous knowledge in setting water quality standards. EPA is therefore, 
without authority to rely on such factors.  
 
V. 
The proposed rule fails to account for the conflict resolution provided by the CWA. 
 
EPA’s regulations under 40 CFR § 131.7 provide a mechanism for resolving disputes between 
States and Indian tribes with TAS status that arise as a result of differing water quality standards 
on common bodies of water. Eligibility for EPA’s § 131.7 dispute resolution depends on six criteria, 
including §131.7(b)(2) which requires the tribe to be “eligible to the same extent as a state for 
purposes of water quality standards,” and §131.7(b)(5) which requires the WQS to not only be 
approved by EPA, but also to be adopted pursuant to the respective state and tribal laws. Tribes 
without TAS authority as required under (b)(2), and tribes that have not adopted EPA’s proposed 
baseline WQS pursuant to that tribe’s laws as required under (b)(5) would appear to fall outside 
the 40 CFR §131.7 dispute mechanism. Will there still be an available avenue to resolve 
prospective state-tribal differences in WQS that result in unreasonable consequences? If not, does 
 
9 The proposed rule creates water quality criteria that maintains water quality at the pour point and “provide[s] for the 
attainment and maintenance of water quality standards” for downstream waters. 88 Fed. Reg. at 29505. Requiring 
maintenance of WQS at these “pour points” will create a patchwork of compliance zones as waters move across Idaho 
lands, into reservations, and out again. The “pour point” language will therefore, dramatically increase the stringency 
of compliance with downstream WQS. The existing approach, requiring WQS compliance at downstream States is 
more diffuse, offers regulatory clarity, and complies with the language of the CWA.  

9 of 9 
 
EPA have the necessary authority to develop appropriate regulations to address disputes over 
baseline WQS? EPA has failed to answer these important questions.  
 
VI. 
The proposed rule circumvents cooperative federalism and usurps the role of Idaho 
under the CWA 
 
The proposed tribal-based WQS requirements infringe upon the distinct and exclusive role of 
States (and authorized tribes) in promulgating WQS for all intrastate waters under the CWA and 
its principles of cooperative federalism. See 33 U.S.C. §§ 1251(b), 1313, 1370; 40 C.F.R. §§ 131.1, 
131.2, 131.3, 131.4, 131.8, 131.10. They are also inconsistent with existing EPA guidance, 
including EPA’s 2000 Human Health Methodology, which gives States the authority and flexibility 
to choose the populations that the States wish to protect and to choose among a range of acceptable 
parameters (including FCRs and cancer risk levels) when developing HHC—which are risk 
management decisions left to the discretion of the States and those tribes with TAS authorization 
for WQS purposes. Under no circumstances should EPA take action that restricts or diminishes in 
any way the role and flexibility of States (and authorized tribes) under the CWA and EPA’s existing 
guidance.  
 
CONCLUSION:  
A Better Way Forward 
 
State WQS apply to all waters of the United States within each State's boundaries, including waters 
on Indian reservations. The EPA recently promulgated rules making TAS application and approval 
easier. While tribes undergo this process, waters on reservations remain covered by State standards.  
 
Idaho has spent more than 30 years establishing science-based WQS that recognize its unique 
topography, hydrology, geology, climate, rivers, streams, and lakes that make up Idaho’s water 
bodies. These standards have been reviewed and approved by EPA on a periodic basis, making 
them CWA-effective. EPA-approved State WQS provide a much stronger baseline for developing 
tribal WQS and are better tailored to fit tribes within Idaho, rather than EPA's proposed broad-
brush. 
 
 
Respectfully, 
 
 
____________________ 
Raul Labrador 
Idaho Attorney General
