Coalition Comments 
 
1 
 
August 3, 2023 
 
The Honorable Radhika Fox  
Assistant Administrator  
Office of Water  
Environmental Protection Agency  
1200 Pennsylvania Avenue, NW  
Washington, DC 20460  
 
 
 
 
Re:  
Proposed Rule, Environmental Protection Agency; Federal Baseline Water Quality 
Standards for Indian Reservations (88 Fed. Reg. 29,496-29,526, May 5, 2023) 
 
 
Dear Assistant Administrator Fox: 
 
The undersigned national trade associations (the Coalition) representing businesses in the 
agriculture, construction, energy, manufacturing, and mining sectors as well as the broad 
business community, appreciate the opportunity to comment on this proposed rule (88 FR 29496 
(May 5, 2023)) and to offer our input. Coalition members would be directly affected by the 
proposed rulemaking through changes to the water quality standards program and associated 
state and tribal treatment as a state (TAS) programs that are likely to result from the imposition 
of national baseline standards, including impairment designations, changes to existing water 
quality criteria, and discharge permit conditions. The Coalition supports sustainable regulation to 
uphold our shared environmental, social, and economic priorities. The Coalition also supports 
efforts to promote tribal self-determination and believes empowering tribes through the 
Congressionally approved TAS process provides the greatest potential for long term water 
quality improvements. Unfortunately, the proposed rule would not achieve these important goals, 
as it is not sustainable or implementable. If finalized as proposed, it will not create the desired 
human health or water quality outcomes. It may also unnecessarily hinder the development of 
our members’ important off-reservation projects that are essential to the nation’s long-term 
social, economic, and environmental needs. 
 
First, the proposal lacks a legal basis under the Clean Water Act (CWA). The authority that the 
Environmental Protection Agency (EPA or the Agency) claims supports the rule has not actually 
been provided by Congress. Second, even if Congress has granted EPA the authority it asserts, 
the proposed framework does not meet the minimum substantive or procedural requirements of 
the CWA or EPA’s water quality standards regulations. Finally, the proposed rule does not 
provide information or explanation sufficient for the public to understand the proposal or to 
make informed comments. For all these reasons, described in detail below, the Coalition urges 
EPA to withdraw the proposed rule. At a bare minimum, EPA must conduct significantly more 
outreach to states, tribes, and the regulated community on the proposed requirements and the 
consequences of their implementation before considering a supplemental proposal.  
 

 Coalition Comments 
 
2 
 
The Coalition appreciates the opportunity to provide these public comments and we look forward 
to further engagement on this important topic. 
 
1. Absent TAS approval, EPA has no authority under CWA section 303(c)(4)(B) to 
establish water quality standards on tribal reservations. 
 
EPA’s purported authority for the proposed rule lies in an Administrator’s Determination that 
was made in 2001, pursuant to CWA section 303(c)(4)(B). However, section 303(c)(4)(B) does 
not authorize EPA to make an Administrator’s Determination for waters on tribal reservations 
where TAS has not been granted. Thus, the 2001 Administrator’s Determination was beyond 
EPA’s authority and can have no legal effect. Even if EPA issued a new Administrator’s 
Determination to support the proposal, to the extent it purported to exert CWA authority over 
non-TAS tribes or reservations, it would be beyond EPA’s authority. The proposal goes well 
beyond what Congress established in the CWA and, if finalized, would be unlawful. 
 
As EPA acknowledges throughout the proposal, CWA section 303 sets out detailed requirements 
for states to establish, maintain, and update water quality standards; to evaluate the quality of its 
waters against those standards; and to develop total maximum daily loads where waters are not 
meeting the applicable standards. Section 303 does not mandate similar actions by tribes. Indeed, 
section 303 does not include a single mention of tribes and therefore does not create any 
mandatory duties for tribes outside of the TAS context. 
 
Section 518 of the CWA was enacted in 1987 to give federally recognized tribes the ability to 
obtain “treatment as a state” (referred to as TAS). Act of Feb. 4, 1987, Pub.L. No. 100-4, tit. V, § 
506, 101 Stat. 76 (codified as amended at 33 U.S.C. § 1377). If a tribe pursues this opportunity 
under CWA section 518, it may take on the same CWA obligations as states including 
establishing water quality standards under section 303, certifying discharges from federally 
permitted projects under section 401, and issuing discharge permits under sections 402 and 404. 
To obtain TAS a tribe must make certain demonstrations mandated by the CWA and EPA 
regulations, develop a program that meets the federal minimum standards, and then obtain EPA’s 
approval. 33 U.S.C. § 1377(e); see also City of Albuquerque v. Browner, 97 F. 3d 415, fn 8 (10th 
Cir. 1996) (“To qualify as a state under the Act, an Indian tribe must submit an application to the 
EPA which includes: a statement that the tribe is recognized by the Secretary of the Interior; a 
descriptive statement demonstrating that the tribal governing body is currently carrying out 
substantial government functions over a defined area; a description of the tribe's authority to 
regulate water quality over certain waters; a description of the tribe's capability to administer an 
effective water quality standards program; and any additional documentation which the 
Administrator deems necessary to support a tribal application. 40 C.F.R. § 131.8 (1995).”). 
 
Pursuant to CWA section 518(e), EPA “is authorized to treat an Indian tribe as a State for 
purposes of [CWA] title II and [CWA] sections 104, 106, 303, 305, 308, 309, 314, 319, 401, 
402, 404 and 406 of this Act to the degree necessary to carry out the objectives of this section, 
but only if…”  the tribe receives TAS authority from EPA. 33 U.S.C. § 1377(e) (emphasis 
added). In other words, CWA section 303 does not apply to tribes or tribal lands unless that tribe 
has obtained TAS authority from EPA. In stark contrast to the text of the CWA, the proposed 

 Coalition Comments 
 
3 
 
baseline water quality standards would apply only to tribal reservations where TAS has not been 
granted. 
 
The proposed rule is contrary to and conflicts with the text and structure of the CWA, including 
sections 303 and 518(e). Congress did not grant EPA authority to regulate tribal reservation 
lands or waters unless those tribes obtain TAS authority under section 518(e). Under the 
proposal, EPA purports to grant itself authority that Congress did not provide in the CWA. If the 
Agency proceeds to finalize this rule, it has an obligation to explain how section 518(e) does not 
preclude this action entirely and explain whether the Agency is relying on a source of authority 
within the CWA other than the 2001 Administrator’s Determination.  
 
2. The proposed framework is unlawful under CWA section 303.  
 
Even if EPA can overcome the foundational lack of legal authority to promulgate this rule, the 
proposed framework and structure create additional and no less significant legal infirmities. 
Although EPA asserts that it is proposing a “baseline water quality standard” for tribal 
reservations, the proposal would actually create an entirely new federal framework that would 
displace state and TAS tribal authority. The proposal undermines the water quality standards 
program and creates significant uncertainty for both the regulated community and regulators. As 
discussed further below, states and TAS tribes are the primary authority for water quality 
standards, not EPA. Even setting that aside, the proposal does not actually propose applicable 
water quality criteria, rather it gives EPA unfettered authority to set numeric criteria in the future 
with no transparency or rulemaking procedure. EPA has not sufficiently explained its rationale 
for the proposal, nor has EPA provided any evidence that Congress intended to create such 
sweeping federal authority when it enacted the CWA. If finalized as proposed, the rule will be 
arbitrary, capricious, and an abuse of discretion. 5 U.S.C. § 706. 
 
a. Congress gave States and TAS tribes primary authority to establish water quality 
standards, not EPA. 
 
Section 303 of the CWA sets out detailed requirements for states to establish, maintain, and 
update water quality standards; to evaluate the quality of its waters against those standards; to 
develop total maximum daily loads where waters are not meeting the applicable standards; and 
implement a continuing planning process to protect and maintain water quality. 33 U.S.C. § 
1313(a), (c), (d), (e); 40 CFR Part 131. As explained above, those same CWA obligations apply 
to tribes, but only after they have received TAS authority. 33 U.S.C. § 1377(e). So, states and 
TAS tribes have obligations to establish, maintain, and update water quality standards under 
CWA section 303. EPA’s role under section 303 is to review state and TAS tribal water quality 
standards, determine if they comply with the requirements of the CWA, and either approve or 
disapprove those standards. 33 U.S.C. § 1313(c)(2)(A); 40 C.F.R. § 131.5(a). If EPA disapproves 
a state or TAS tribal standard, EPA is required to propose a federal standard that complies with 
the CWA. 33 U.S.C. §1313(c)(3); 40 C.F.R. § 131.5(b). EPA’s role under section 303 is plainly 
one of oversight—to ensure that state and TAS tribal water quality standards comply with the 
CWA. Nothing in the CWA indicates that Congress intended CWA section 303(c)(4)(B) to 
convert EPA’s oversight authority into primary authority over tribal reservation lands where no 
TAS authority has been granted.  

 Coalition Comments 
 
4 
 
 
EPA’s role in promulgating water quality standards in CWA Section 303(c)(4)(B) is secondary 
to the states’ role. See 33 U.S.C. § 1313(a), (c)(1), (c)(2)(A); see City of Arcadia v. U.S. Env't 
Prot. Agency, 411 F.3d 1103, 1106 (9th Cir. 2005) (“The CWA operates on a principle of 
cooperative federalism where states take the lead in setting [water quality standards].”); Natural 
Res. Def. Council, Inc. v. EPA, 16 F.3d 1395, 1401 (4th Cir. 1993) (“While the states and EPA 
share duties in achieving [the CWA’s] goal, primary responsibility for establishing appropriate 
water quality standards is left to the states.  EPA sits in a reviewing capacity of the state-
implemented standards, with approval and rejection powers only” (citations omitted).)  EPA’s 
role under CWA section 303(c) is limited to serving as a “back stop” when states fail to submit 
water quality standards that comply with federal standards or otherwise violate the CWA. Gulf 
Restoration Network v. McCarthy, 783 F.3d 227, 231 (5th Cir. 2015). This oversight or backstop 
authority applies to tribes only after EPA has granted TAS authority and the tribe has affirmative 
responsibility to establish water quality standards that comply with the CWA. EPA has not 
provided any explanation or legal analysis that would overcome this and other longstanding 
precedent that confirms the primary role of states and TAS tribes to establish water quality 
standards, and the limited role for EPA to provide oversight where a state or TAS tribe fails in its 
CWA obligations.  
 
EPA’s own water quality standards handbook reinforces EPA’s understanding of its limited role 
to ensure that states and TAS tribes meet their obligations under the CWA. See, e.g., U.S. EPA 
Water Quality Standards Handbook (2017), section 1.5 (“The CWA requires states and 
authorized tribes to adopt new or revised WQS and then submit them to the EPA for review and 
approval or disapproval action. The EPA has the authority and duty under CWA Section 
303(c)(3) to approve or disapprove new or revised WQS.”). When a state or TAS tribe fails in its 
duties and EPA disapproves a water quality standard, section 303(c)(4)(A) requires EPA to 
promulgate a replacement federal water quality standard that is consistent with the CWA.  Id. at 
section 1.5.2 (“In the case of disapproval, the EPA must promptly propose federal WQS under 
Section 303(c)(4)(A) and promulgate such WQS within 90 days of proposal, provided that the 
state or tribe does not make appropriate corrections within 90 days. The EPA may approve some 
provisions and not others within the same WQS submission.”). Section 303(c)(4)(B) similarly 
allows EPA to step in when a state or TAS tribe is not performing its obligations under the 
CWA. This section of the CWA is intended, and has been used by EPA for 50 years, to authorize 
the Agency to determine, in specific cases, that a new or revised water quality standard is 
necessary. Id. at section 1.5.2 (“The EPA may also promulgate a new or revised federal WQS 
where the Administrator determines under Section 303(c)(4)(B) that such a WQS is necessary to 
meet the requirements of the CWA, and no WQS have been submitted for the EPA to disapprove 
under Section 303(c)(4)(A). This situation could occur, for instance, if the EPA determines that 
particular WQS provisions are missing from a state’s or tribe’s currently applicable WQS 
or that the current WQS do not otherwise meet the requirements of the CWA.”) (emphasis 
added). 
  
For the first time in 50 years, the proposed rule would authorize EPA to set nationally applicable 
water quality standards based on newly asserted authority in 303(c)(4)(B) that is untethered from 

 Coalition Comments 
 
5 
 
its oversight role of state and TAS tribal programs.1 This would be a remarkable expansion of 
EPA’s authority and raises concerns that after more than five decades administering the CWA, 
EPA has now “‘claimed to discover in a long-extant statute an unheralded power’ representing a 
‘transformative expansion in its regulatory authority,’” W. Virginia v. Env't Prot. Agency, 142 S. 
Ct. 2587, 2610 (2022), which has never applied nationwide.  EPA’s reliance on section 
303(c)(4)(B), 33 U.S.C. § 1313(c)(4)(B), as support for expanding its authority to promulgate 
nationally applicable water quality standards has parallels to the Agency’s invocation of 
“newfound power in the vague language of an ancillary provision of the Act” that the Supreme 
Court rejected in W. Virginia.  Id.; see also Biden v. Nebraska, 600 U.S. __, 143 S. Ct. 2355, 
2373 (2023) (discussing W. Virginia).     
 
In this case, the statute clearly provides states and TAS tribes with the primary role of 
establishing water quality standards and limits EPA’s role to oversight of those programs. Yet, 
EPA’s new interpretation of section 303(c)(4)(B) would authorize the Agency to promulgate 
nationally applicable water quality standards outside of its oversight role. EPA’s assertion of this 
new and extraordinary authority could be used to completely overtake established state and TAS 
tribal water quality standards programs, upending the cooperative federalism foundation of the 
CWA.  
 
The proposed rule’s departure from longstanding Agency practice also raises concerns in light of 
Sackett v. EPA, 598 U.S. ___, 143 S. Ct. 1322 (May 25, 2023) and Solid Waste Agency of 
Northern Cook Cty. v. Army Corps of Engineers, 531 U.S. 159, 168 (2001) (SWANCC).  In both 
cases, the Supreme Court rejected an interpretation of a provision of the CWA that would 
“significantly alter the balance between federal and state power and the power of the 
Government over private property.”  Sackett, 143 S. Ct. at 1341 (quoting United States Forest 
Service v. Cowpasture River Preservation Assn., 590 U. S. ___, 140 S.Ct. 1837, 1849-1850 
(2020)); see also SWANCC, 531 U.S. at 172-74.  The Court requires a clear statement of 
congressional intent before assuming that Congress would have delegated authority to the 
Agency to alter that balance. 143 S. Ct. at 1341-42; see also SWANCC, 531 U.S. at 172-74. No 
such clear statement is present here. 
  
Indeed, the CWA is clear that states and TAS tribes have primary authority to establish, 
maintain, and update water quality standards. EPA may promulgate federal water quality 
standards, but only if a state or TAS tribe establishes standards that do not comply with the CWA 
or if they fail to establish required standards altogether. Yet the proposed rule would 
significantly expand EPA’s authority, allowing the Agency to promulgate nationally applicable 
water quality standards outside of its oversight authority, and on tribal reservations where a tribe 
 
1 EPA began to pursue the prospect of claiming this novel authority in 2001 when the EPA 
Administrator signed a proposed rule with an Administrator’s Determination that tribal baseline 
standards are “necessary.” 88 FR at 29499. However, the Agency never published its proposed 
rule in the Federal Register or otherwise presented its position or analysis in writing for public 
comment, so the Agency’s legal analysis was never tested through public comment, APA 
rulemaking, or judicial review. As such, for all intents and purposes, this proposed rule is the 
first time EPA’s interpretation of 303(c)(4)(B) has been presented to the public for consideration 
and comment. 

 Coalition Comments 
 
6 
 
has no TAS authority. The proposed rule is well beyond the authority Congress provided to EPA 
in the CWA and therefore cannot be finalized. 
 
b. The proposed narrative “water quality criteria” does not satisfy the requirements 
of EPA’s existing regulations. 
 
EPA’s regulations at 40 CFR Part 131 provide substantive and procedural requirements that 
states and TAS tribes must meet when promulgating water quality standards. In addition to 
lacking authority or precedent under the CWA, EPA’s proposed rule also does not comport with 
the Agency’s own regulations and requirements.   
 
As the proposal correctly explains, water quality standards have three components: 1) designated 
uses; 2) applicable criteria; and 3) antidegradation policies. 88 FR at 29497. But EPA’s proposed 
rule only presents designated uses, antidegradation policies, and placeholder narrative standards. 
Pursuant to the proposal, the applicable CWA criteria—the numeric criteria generated through 
the “binding translations”—will be established at some point in the future, by an EPA Regional 
Administrator, without any rulemaking procedures or transparency. Proposed 131.XX (d)(2). 
This feature of the proposal is contrary to both the substantive and procedural requirements in 
EPA’s regulations. Nothing in the CWA authorizes EPA to circumvent its own regulatory 
requirements or its Administrative Procedure Act (APA) notice and comment rulemaking 
obligations as EPA’s proposal would allow.  
 
EPA’s existing regulations also require states and TAS tribes to submit water quality standards 
packages for EPA approval. These packages must include use designations, the methods used 
and analyses conducted to support the water quality standards revisions, water quality criteria 
sufficient to protect the designated uses, an antidegradation policy, certification by the state 
Attorney General or other appropriate legal authority within the state that the water quality 
standards were duly adopted pursuant to state law, and other general information “which will aid 
the Agency in determining the adequacy of the scientific basis of the standards…as well as 
information on general policies applicable to state standards which may affect their application 
and implementation.” 40 CFR § 131.6 (a)-(f). Yet the proposed rule itself does not meet these 
minimum requirements, which are required of states and TAS tribes. The proposal provides no 
description of methods or analysis that EPA utilized to support the proposed standards, and it 
fails to include the actual water quality criteria that will be applicable where the rule would 
apply. The proposal also does not include any information to aid the public in understanding “the 
adequacy of the scientific basis of the standards…as well as information on general policies 
applicable to state standards which may affect their application and implementation” as required 
in 40 CFR § 131.6(a)-(f). Based on the lack of analysis, including a lack of explanation of the 
supposed scientific basis for the proposed rule, it appears that EPA would hold states and TAS 
tribes to a far higher standard than EPA is setting for itself.  
 
The proposed rule fails to comply with EPA’s own substantive and procedural requirements for 
water quality standards and submissions by states and TAS tribes and is therefore arbitrary and 
capricious and an unlawful exercise of agency discretion.   
 

 Coalition Comments 
 
7 
 
c. The proposed “binding translations” are an unlawful backdoor to establish 
numeric criteria without the required rulemaking. 
 
Existing EPA regulations also set forth the form that water quality criteria must take. The 
regulations provide that states and TAS tribes should “Establish numerical values based on: (i) 
304(a) Guidance, (ii) or 304(a) Guidance modified to reflect site-specific conditions; or (iii) 
Other scientifically defensible methods” and states should “Establish narrative criteria or criteria 
based on biomonitoring methods where numerical criteria cannot be established or to supplement 
numerical criteria.” 40 CFR § 131.11(b).  
 
The proposed rule purports to establish narrative criteria for tribal reservation waters covered by 
the proposal. But the proposal does not state whether the narrative criteria are being established 
because numerical criteria “cannot be established,” as EPA’s regulation directs. More 
importantly, EPA admits that the proposed narrative criteria will not be the applicable criteria for 
CWA purposes. Rather, the actual applicable criteria that will be used to develop permit 
discharge limits, evaluate waters for impairments, and that will be used to establish TMDLs, will 
be based on the “binding translation methods” that are to be established in the future without the 
required APA rulemaking. 88 FR at 29504, 29506, 29508; Proposed 131.XX (d). 
 
The proposed rule states, “The Regional Administrator shall utilize one of the options set forth in 
this paragraph (d)(2) to derive numeric translations of the narrative criteria in paragraph (d)(1) of 
this section for all purposes under the [CWA] section 303(c) for specific parameters as 
necessary to protect applicable designated uses…” Proposed 131.XX (d) (emphasis added). To 
be clear, under the proposed rule EPA Regional Administrators are not authorized to administer 
or implement the narrative criteria that EPA has presented for public comment. Rather, the 
Regional Administrators are required to translate the narrative criteria into numeric criteria, and 
those are the applicable criteria that will be used “for all purposes under the CWA.” Id. The 
proposed narrative criteria are just a stalking horse and the real CWA criteria will be the numeric 
limits that are later developed with “binding translations” that are established without public 
participation or APA rulemaking. 
 
The proposal presents five options available to Regional Administrators to conduct the “binding 
translations” of the narrative criteria: 1) use EPA’s CWA 304(a) recommended criteria, 2) use 
EPA’s CWA 304(a) recommended criteria modified to reflect site-specific conditions, 3) use 
water quality standards “adopted by the Tribe or CWA-effective water quality standards 
applicable in an adjacent or other relevant state(s) or Tribe(s) that are based on sound scientific 
rationale…”, 4) where applicable use provisions to be protective of the Great Lakes System, and 
5) if options 1-4 are not applicable, the Regional Administrator “shall rely on existing CWA 
implementation provisions” to derive numeric translations. Proposed 131.XX (d)(2). Translation 
options 1-3 are seriously flawed and must not be finalized.2  
 
2 Proposed translation options 4 and 5 are based on existing EPA procedures, both formal and 
informal. It should be noted that just because a procedure or practice is existing or longstanding, 
even if it has been promulgated by rule, does not mean it is lawful or consistent with the CWA. 
To the extent that existing procedures authorize states, TAS tribes or EPA to establish generally 
applicable numeric water quality criteria without going through APA rulemaking, those 

 Coalition Comments 
 
8 
 
 
Tellingly, translation options 1 and 2 are taken directly from EPA’s existing regulations that 
instruct states how to establish numeric water quality standards that would be promulgated 
and submitted for EPA review under the CWA. See 40 CFR § 131.11(b) (states should 
“Establish numerical values based on: (i) 304(a) Guidance, (ii) or 304(a) Guidance modified to 
reflect site-specific conditions…”). Under CWA section 304(a), Congress instructed EPA to 
develop criteria that reflects the latest scientific knowledge that could be provided to states and 
TAS tribes as recommendations for how to develop their own numeric criteria.3 EPA’s section 
304(a) criteria are highly complex and technical, and they are not self-implementing. When a 
state or TAS tribe adopts 304(a) criteria, or a modified version based on site-specific conditions, 
it must do so through a rulemaking process and then submit those criteria for EPA approval 
before they are effective for CWA purposes. 40 CFR Part 131. Under the proposed rule, 
translation options 1 and 2 give EPA Regional Administrators the opportunity to unilaterally 
administer these complex, and often controversial,4 section 304(a) criteria on tribal lands by 
calling them a “translation” of narrative criteria. Even more disconcerting, Regional 
Administrators are given the option of determining whether and how to modify the section 
304(a) criteria based on site-specific criteria, and the proposal makes no effort to explain the 
scope or extent of the “site” or the process EPA would undertake to develop “site-specific” 
criteria. Again, this is a process, when undertaken by a state or a TAS tribe that can take years of 
public engagement and rulemaking. Under the proposal all of this would be done by the EPA 
Regional Administrators without any public notice, comment, stakeholder engagement, or 
rulemaking procedures.  
 
Translation option 3 is an obvious and unlawful work-around of EPA’s own TAS program that 
would seriously undermine the CWA. As EPA knows, tribes that wish to administer a water 
quality standards program must receive TAS authority, and then its water quality standards must 
be established through a public process and approved by EPA, just like a state. Only after these 
rigorous procedures will a water quality standard become effective for CWA purposes. However, 
under the proposal, an EPA Regional Administrator could “translate” baseline narrative criteria 
using a non-TAS, non-CWA tribal standard that has not gone through any public process and 
that has not been subject to EPA review. Under the proposal, that tribal standard would be 
selected by the EPA Regional Administrator and automatically become the applicable criteria 
“for all purposes under the CWA.”  
 
procedures are legally vulnerable and should be reconsidered and made consistent with the CWA 
and APA rulemaking requirements.  
3 https://www.epa.gov/wqc/basic-information-water-quality-criteria 
4 As an example, in 2015 the State of Washington adopted EPA’s 304(a) human health criteria, 
modified with state-specific information, after a years-long state rulemaking process. In 2016, 
EPA partially disapproved the state’s criteria, asserting it did not meet the CWA requirements, 
and published federal criteria in its place. In 2019, EPA reconsidered its prior disapproval, 
approved Washington’s 2015 criteria, and withdrew the federal criteria. Then in 2021, EPA 
again reconsidered its prior decision, rescinded the approval of Washington’s 2015 criteria, and 
repromulgated federal criteria that would apply in Washington.  

 Coalition Comments 
 
9 
 
 
EPA acknowledges in the proposal that it is concerned that more tribes have not obtained TAS, 
and its apparent solution is to allow tribes to set water quality criteria applicable for CWA 
purposes without the TAS process.  But EPA’s frustration is not a valid basis to create new 
authority that would be needed for this proposed regulation to be legal. The proposed regulatory 
text underscores the illegality of EPA’s proposed rule—under translation option 3, the Regional 
Administrator can choose either a standard “adopted by a tribe” or a “CWA-effective water 
quality standard” from another state or TAS tribe. Footnote 5 of the proposed rule preamble also 
confirms that some tribes may have water quality standards established under tribal law, but 
“Such standards are not effective for CWA purposes, however, until they are approved by EPA. 
40 CFR 131.21.” 88 FR at 29498. EPA has not provided any explanation or rationale for why a 
non-TAS, non-CWA tribal standard that would not otherwise be effective for CWA purposes, 
could be used as a “translation” under the proposal and become the CWA-applicable water 
quality standard, all without any public process or demonstration that the tribal standard meets 
the requirements of the CWA and EPA’s regulations.  
 
To further confirm that the establishment of the truly applicable numeric water quality criteria 
would be done without any public input or process, proposed section 40 CFR 131.XX (d)(vi) 
requires EPA Regional Administrators to “maintain and make available to the public for 
informational purposes a list of numeric translations…” 88 FR at 29523 (emphasis added). In 
other words, if a person might be subject to one or more translated numeric standards, those 
standards would be available for review on EPA’s website after they are established, but a person 
would have no opportunity to review or comment on proposed translations. Because they were 
not promulgated under the APA, the EPA may even argue that the public could not challenge 
these numeric criteria as final agency actions under the APA, despite their use for all CWA 
purposes. EPA does not have the authority to exempt itself from the APA’s requirements and 
achieve such a result by fiat. 
 
The foregoing demonstrates that the proposed narrative criteria are merely placeholders for 
future numeric criteria that EPA would establish and administer through its own internal 
“binding translation” process. The proposed rule indicates that some aspects of the proposal are 
intended increase transparency for stakeholders (88 FR at 29504); however, the proposed 
“binding translation” framework would significantly undermine transparency and prevent 
stakeholders from participating in the water quality standards setting process. The proposed rule 
is arbitrary, capricious, and unlawful. 
 
d. The rationale supporting the translation framework is lacking and insufficient for 
APA purposes. 
 
As EPA explains, the Agency’s preference is for “Tribes to obtain TAS and develop WQS 
tailored to the Tribe’s individual environmental goals and reservation waters.” 88 FR at 29499. 
However, because the Agency has concluded that allowing that CWA-authorized and mandated 
process to occur would take too long,5 EPA proposes to “rely[] on its role as both the 
 
5 EPA asserts that following the normal CWA processes would “further delay needed water 
quality protections,” but the proposed rule lacks any description of this purported need for the 

 Coalition Comments 
 
10 
 
promulgating entity and the primary implementing authority” to circumvent the CWA process 
and create a new process that would “allow a degree of site-specific tailoring within the 
regulatory construct of the WQS during subsequent implementation of the proposed rule.” 88 FR 
at 29499. The level of “tailoring” that the proposal would allow during implementation (without 
any transparency or public process) is unprecedented. Indeed, we are not aware that any state or 
TAS tribe has ever been authorized to conduct the level of site-specific “tailoring” that EPA 
would authorize for itself in the proposed rule.  
 
In an example of how the proposal allows “tailoring” during implementation, EPA explains that 
“a particular Tribe’s fish consumption rates could be used during the implementation stage as 
part of the calculation to set the appropriate human health criteria value for its waters.”  88 FR at 
29499. This example is concerning, for several reasons.  
 
First, the proposal suggests that setting the “appropriate human health criteria value” is 
something that occurs during the implementation stage. This is inaccurate and does not seem to 
make logical sense. Setting the appropriate human health criteria value is done during the criteria 
development stage—the human health criteria value is part of the water quality criteria—and 
thus the criteria value cannot be set during implementation of the criteria. Second, according to 
the proposal, setting a fish consumption rate is merely an implementation step that can be done 
after criteria is established. From a criteria-setting perspective this also does not seem to add up, 
particularly because EPA has disapproved state-promulgated human health criteria based on 
EPA’s belief that the fish consumption rate selected by the state was not protective enough.6 In 
other words, EPA has historically viewed a fish consumption rate as an integral part of a state 
water quality standard, providing an independent basis to reject a state’s water quality standards 
package. The proposed rule represents significant departures from EPA’s prior statements 
concerning fish consumption and its prior actions on state-promulgated standards. EPA has not 
identified these significant policy changes and has not provided any explanation or rationale to 
support the changes, which seem unreasonable on their face and thus require a careful, 
persuasive explanation that takes account of all relevant factors.  
 
The proposal also does not explain, or even acknowledge, that changing a fish consumption 
rate—by itself—has the potential to change the stringency of a water quality standard by orders 
of magnitude. With all else equal, a human health criterion based on EPA’s default fish 
 
water quality protections. EPA has not cited specific waters that are in need of protections, 
scenarios where specific EPA-issued NPDES permits are not protective because a lack of tribal 
water quality standards, or specific situations that demand greater federal regulatory control over 
tribal lands. It is not enough to state that there is a lack of water quality standards, EPA must 
demonstrate that there is a widespread need for additional protections and that the proposed 
baseline standards would provide those protections.  
6 See e.g., EPA’s February 2, 2015 action disapproving certain water quality standards in Maine 
(“EPA is disapproving Maine’s human health criteria because they are not protective of human 
health for the target population. They are based on a fish consumption rate of 32.4 grams per 
day, with the exception of arsenic which is based on 138 grams per day. However, the Wabanaki 
study indicates that consumption values between 286 and 514 grams per day represent the 
sustenance fishing use in tribal waters.”) 

 Coalition Comments 
 
11 
 
consumption rate of 22 grams per day could be 10 times less stringent than a criterion based on a 
site-specific fish consumption rate of 220 grams per day. This is just one example of where a 
Regional Administrator’s decisions during implementation could have profound implications on 
the applicable water quality criteria value and the resulting permitting and waterbody impairment 
designations both within and in the vicinity of reservations. We are not aware that EPA has ever 
authorized a state or TAS tribe to make changes during “implementation” that would result in 
such significant changes in stringency to a water quality criterion. EPA has not explained why 
allowing such significant changes during “implementation” of the proposed rule would be 
appropriate, lawful, or consistent with the CWA or the APA, especially without any public 
process, input, or rulemaking.  
 
Finally, the proposal does not explain how the binding translations could affect upstream or 
neighboring state or tribal TAS water quality standards. For example, if the baseline narrative 
criteria are translated into very stringent numeric criteria based on a site-specific fish 
consumption rate, it is possible that EPA could determine that an existing EPA-approved state or 
TAS tribal water quality standard is no longer protective of the downstream “binding 
translation.” The proposal does not explain how EPA will engage with upstream states or TAS 
tribes in these circumstances, particularly since the translations will be done without public 
notice and APA rulemaking, or whether EPA will mandate those upstream standards be modified 
to account for the new “binding translations” established by EPA Regional Administrators. 
Without significantly more explanation and analysis, the proposed rule does not satisfy EPA’s 
obligations under the APA and is arbitrary and capricious.  
 
3. The proposal improperly conflates aquatic life criteria and human health criteria and does 
so to further impose EPA’s flawed construct of “unsuppressed” fish consumption. 
 
The proposed rule establishes a designated use for aquatic life as follows, “Protection and 
propagation of fish, shellfish, and wildlife, which includes protection of human health of 
consumers of fish, shellfish, and other aquatic life.” Proposed 131.XX (c)(1). This proposed 
designated use improperly conflates concepts of aquatic life and human health criteria and 
introduces significant uncertainty about the meaning and reach of an aquatic life-based water 
quality standard.  
 
On its website, EPA explains that aquatic life water quality criteria are based on “how much of a 
chemical can be present in surface water before it is likely to harm plant and animal life. EPA 
designs aquatic life criteria to protect both freshwater and saltwater organisms from short-term 
and long-term exposure.”7 This is what is traditionally thought of as aquatic life water quality 
criteria—ensuring the water quality is sufficient to protect the species that live within that water 
body or category of water bodies (e.g., cold water fishery). 
 
Also on its website, EPA explains that human health criteria are based on “how much of a 
specific chemical can be present in surface water before it is likely to harm human health.”8 
 
7 https://www.epa.gov/wqc/aquatic-life-criteria-and-methods-toxics 
8 https://www.epa.gov/wqc/human-health-water-quality-criteria-and-methods-toxics 

 Coalition Comments 
 
12 
 
Again, this is commonly how the regulated community and delegated state and TAS tribes think 
of as human health criteria—ensuring that the fish and drinking water consumed by humans is 
not harmful to human health.  
 
Under the proposed rule, water quality criteria protective of the “aquatic life” designated use 
must also protect “human health of consumers of fish, shellfish, and other aquatic life.” This 
designated use definition is novel, conflates two traditional water quality criteria, will make 
implementation confusing, and makes any final rule vulnerable to litigation. 
 
First, given the structure of the proposal one must assume that the aquatic life designated use is 
intentionally over-broad. As written, it would allow an EPA Regional Administrator to replace 
the promulgated narrative standard with numeric “translations” based on aquatic life 304(a) 
criteria or human health 304(a) criteria or a combination of both. This creates maximum 
flexibility for EPA in its unlawful “tailoring” where it need not explain whether its translation is 
based on numeric aquatic life or human health criteria, or why, and where EPA is insulated from 
public scrutiny and legal challenges. The flip side is that this novel and overly broad aquatic life 
designated use, coupled with the proposed “binding translation” procedure provides essentially 
zero transparency for the public, the regulated community or states or TAS tribes. This could not 
have been what Congress intended when it enacted CWA section 303(c)(4)(B). 
 
Second, bootstrapping human health criteria into the aquatic life designated use opens the door 
for EPA to include in the proposal the concept of “unsuppressed” fish consumption rates, like it 
did in the proposed tribal reserved rights rule just a few short months ago. 87 FR 74361 (Dec. 5, 
2022). But in this proposal, EPA directs itself (as opposed to states) to utilize unsuppressed fish 
consumption rates as part of its “tailoring” of the national baseline standards to be tribe-specific. 
EPA provides no explanation for why it should include a fish consumption rate (or rate of 
consumption for other aquatic resources) based on an estimate of “unsuppressed” consumption 
instead of on actual, real-world consumption data or observations. Indeed, just four years ago in 
its 2019 approval of Idaho’s water quality standards EPA stated that “[n]othing in the CWA or 
the EPA’s regulations and guidance, including the 2000 Methodology, requires a state to set a 
FCR based on an estimate of unsuppressed consumption.”  EPA’s Approval of Idaho’s New and 
Revised Human Health Water Quality Criteria for Toxics and Other Water Quality Standards 
Provisions at 12 (Apr. 4, 2019).  EPA’s proposal provides no detailed or persuasive rationale for 
departing from this recent interpretation, and it does not identify any provisions of the CWA that 
would require EPA to employ a counterfactual estimate of “unsuppressed” consumption. 
Furthermore, it is inconsistent with a sound scientific rationale to assume conditions such as 
subsistence levels of consumption that are neither reflective of the world as it is today nor 
grounded in a fact-based, reasonably foreseeable estimate of the future. The Supreme Court has 
recently disapproved the loose interpretation of tribal treaties that EPA has previously used to 
justify “unsuppressed” fish consumption rates. See Arizona v. Navajo Nation, No. 21-1484, slip 
op. (June 22, 2023). 
 
Lastly, EPA provides no guidance for how aquatic life water quality criteria should be or must be 
protective of fish consumers. Must the aquatic life criteria ensure that there is a sufficient 
quantity of fish in the water to support all consumers, or all subsistence consumers? If so, the 
proposal provides no information concerning how such an analysis would be conducted. Will 

 Coalition Comments 
 
13 
 
EPA consider other aspects of its human health criteria when establishing an aquatic life 
criterion—like the lifetime average rate of water consumption, or other exposures accounted for 
through application of the relative risk contribution? EPA has not provided sufficient information 
on this aspect of the proposed rule to allow the public to submit informed comments. If the 
Agency wishes to pursue this novel designated use, it must provide a supplemental notice that 
explains the purpose of the hybrid aquatic life-human health designated use, how such a use 
could be protected, how protective criteria would be developed within the water quality 
standards program, and how EPA’s APA obligations will be satisfied during the development of 
applicable numeric criteria.9  
 
EPA’s failure to consider or address these basic questions runs afoul of the Agency’s obligations 
under the APA.  
 
4. The proposed cultural and traditional uses must be defined for clarity, transparency, and 
implementation purposes. 
 
In proposed section 131.XX(c)(3), EPA has proposed a general designated use that involves 
protection of the cultural and traditional uses of tribal reservation waters.  While respect for such 
considerations is certainly appropriate, EPA has declined to provide any specificity around what 
“cultural and traditional uses” may include, suggesting it could encompass anything from aquatic 
life protections for grasses used for basket weaving to primary contact protection for full body 
submersion done during rituals. EPA explains that it cannot define “cultural and traditional uses” 
with any specificity because “they can include a variety of uses specific to the ceremonies and 
traditions of each Tribe, and each use may require different levels of protection.” 88 FR at 
29503. The proposed rule essentially provides an open invitation to set water quality criteria at 
almost any value that is perceived to be protective of these general and undefined uses, without 
any transparency or rulemaking, and with no demonstration that such criteria would be 
scientifically sound or defensible.  Here again, the potential implementation challenges are 
ignored both for the permitting authorities and dischargers. 
 
5. The antidegradation policy must be streamlined and clarified. 
 
EPA has proposed a lengthy antidegradation policy and implementation methods that would 
create new uncertainty for anyone proposing to conduct activities within or near tribal 
reservation waters. The proposed scope of applicability, how specific parameters that would be 
considered to determine if water is high quality, and the “area in which the waters are located” 
are all left unclear and too vague to allow for transparent implementation. EPA must streamline 
and clarify the antidegradation policy before finalizing the rule. 
 
The proposal states that the antidegradation policy applies to “all requests to authorize new or 
expanded regulated activities” and that term includes, but is not limited to, “any activity that 
 
9 If EPA moves forward with a supplemental notice, the Agency should incorporate probabilistic 
risk assessment (PRA) as a modern approach to risk assessment that has the ability to address the 
risk of vulnerable sub-populations in a granular, transparent, and highly data driven manner. 
Please review and consider the attached February 28, 2023 NCASI memorandum. 

 Coalition Comments 
 
14 
 
requires a permit, license, or water quality certification pursuant to sections 401, 402 and 404 of 
the CWA.” 88 FR at 29523. It is unclear what other regulatory activities would authorize 
discharges to waters and therefore trigger the antidegradation policy. The proposal should be 
revised to include an exhaustive list of regulatory actions that would trigger the antidegradation 
policy and examples of activities that would not trigger antidegradation. 
 
The proposal also states that EPA will identify high quality waters “on a parameter-by-parameter 
basis” and “[a] parameter is high quality if its water quality level exceeds its water quality 
criterion in paragraph (d) of this section.” It is unclear what specific parameters EPA will 
consider and if EPA is intending to evaluate these parameters against the narrative criteria in the 
proposal or, more likely, if EPA is intending to use the applicable numeric criteria that would 
later be established through the “binding translation” process. The proposal must be revised to 
explain what parameters EPA intends to consider and if EPA intends to compare them against 
the proposed narrative criteria or the numeric translations that will be established later. Notably, 
if EPA intends the latter, it is more evidence that the proposed narrative criteria will not be the 
applicable CWA criteria, but that the numeric criteria resulting from the “binding translations” 
will become the criteria effective for “all purposes under the [CWA]” without any notice or 
comment rulemaking. 
 
The proposal also does not attempt to establish the geographic area that will be considered in an 
antidegradation analysis. Under the proposal, a socio-economic analysis is conducted for the 
“area in which the waters are located” to determine if a lowering of water quality is “necessary to 
accommodate important economic or social development.” 88 FR at 29523. However, under the 
proposal, “the ‘area in which the waters are located’ shall be determined on a case-by-case basis 
and shall include all areas directly impacted by the proposed regulatory activity.” 88 FR at 
29524. This broad and undefined term creates significant uncertainty and could allow EPA to 
evaluate an expansive geographic area, including one that extends beyond the tribal reservation.  
 
The antidegradation policy in the proposed rule must be streamlined and revised to provide 
greater clarity and certainty. 
 
6. EPA’s proposed process for nominating a water as an Outstanding National Resource 
Water (ONRW) is unreasonably incomplete at best and unlawful at worst. 
 
EPA has proposed that, under its antidegradation policy, any person or entity could nominate a 
water for ONRW status by submitting the nomination in writing with an explanation of “why” 
the water should be designated as an ONRW. In a proposed rule that sets out numerous factors 
for EPA to consider during implementation, the ONRW provision includes not a single factor or 
criterion that will be considered by EPA when determining if a water should be an ONRW.  
 
An ONRW watershed would have extremely limited, and perhaps zero, development 
opportunities because there can be no lowering of water quality within the water. However, EPA 
has not set out any factors or criteria that would inform whether a water should be an ONRW. 
EPA has not even required that a nomination include a map or location coordinates for the 
nominated water or segment.  
 

 Coalition Comments 
 
15 
 
EPA’s proposal is inadequate and must be revised to include specific criteria that will be 
considered by the EPA Regional Administrator before concluding that a water should be an 
ONRW. Factors should include but not be limited to: exact boundaries of the proposed water or 
segment; chemical data; biological data; ecological value, including whether and to what extent 
the water can and does support endangered or threatened aquatic life; flow data to ensure there is 
a consistent presence of water within the feature; use data to understand the current economic or 
aesthetic values of the feature; a description of the exceptional aesthetic, cultural or historical 
value of the water; whether the water is located in a national or state park, wildlife refuge or 
wilderness areas, special trout waters, federal Wild and Scenic Rivers; and whether the water has 
been significantly modified by human activities.  
More broadly, an ONRW designation has the potential to significantly affect economic 
development activities on tribal lands and upstream of reservations. If the standard is that no 
change in water quality is allowed in an ONRW, taken to its logical extreme this could mean that 
development activities could be precluded for tens or hundreds of miles upstream of the 
designated ONRW. This could in turn lead to severe restrictions or even preclusion of the 
important projects our members represent if even microscopic changes in water quality could be 
detected. Without proper regulatory guidelines setting out the level of analysis and factual 
support required to obtain an ONRW designation, extraordinary burdens could be placed on 
upstream discharges to show no effects on the ONRW. These challenges are why states have 
been very cautious in using ONRW designations; yet the proposal would seem to open the door 
to ONRW designations without any guidelines or sideboards at all. This is another case where 
the broad implications of the proposed rule have not been acknowledged by EPA or considered 
during this rulemaking process. 
Given the significant use and development restrictions that result from designation as an ONRW, 
and the potentially significant economic consequences that could result from improper or 
arbitrary designations, EPA must put more specific parameters and criteria in place to ensure that 
only waters that are truly deserving of the ONRW title receive it. Additionally, any ONRW 
designation by EPA must be supported by information demonstrating that the resource is in fact 
an outstanding national resource for which the benefits of protecting it from any degradation 
outweigh the economic and societal costs. Without more specific factors, parameters or criteria 
provided for ONRW designations, the ONRW nomination and determination process in the 
proposed rule would be arbitrary and capricious.  
 
7. EPA has failed to reasonably consider and weigh the potential impacts of the proposed 
rule, including costs. 
 
In the economic analysis section of the proposal preamble, EPA states the following: 
“The baseline WQS proposed in this rule would not themselves impose costs on any 
entity.” 88 FR at 29518. Yet, EPA elsewhere acknowledges that the rule in fact could 
lead to additional compliance costs for regulated entities, and the Agency prepared an 
economic analysis “to estimate the costs that could arise as a result of promulgating 
baseline WQS and identify the potential benefits and beneficiaries of the baseline WQS.” 
Economic Analysis for Proposed Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian 
Reservations, Prepared for United States Environmental Protection Agency Office of 

 Coalition Comments 
 
16 
 
Water, Office of Science and Technology (April 5, 2023), at p. 1 (Economic Analysis). 
EPA also acknowledged limitations of its analysis. Economic Analysis, at pp. 49-51 
(April 5, 2023) (emphasis added) (noting that “little information is available on the cost 
of P2 [pollution prevention] programs for individual pollutants,” stringent control options 
such as granular activated carbon or reverse osmosis may be insufficient “in meeting 
potential effluent limitations” as a result of the rule, and dischargers “would likely pursue 
some form of relief from the requirements” such as a variance.)  
 
In a line of cases from Motor Vehicle Mfrs. Ass’n, Inc. v. State Farm Mut. Auto. Ins. Co., 
463 U.S. 29 (1983) to Entergy Corp. v. Riverkeeper, 556 U.S. 208 (2009) to Michigan v. 
EPA, 135 S. Ct. 2699 (2015), the Supreme Court has made clear that agencies such as 
EPA must weigh cost and benefits, absent a statutory prohibition to the contrary. In 
Michigan, the Court made clear that statutory language such as “appropriate and 
necessary” is the kind of “capacious” language that requires the consideration of all 
relevant factors, including cost. See Michigan, 135 S. Ct. at 2707. All nine justices agreed 
that “[c]ost is almost always a relevant, and usually, a highly important factor in 
regulation. Unless Congress provides otherwise, an agency acts unreasonably in 
establishing a standard-setting process that ignore[s] economic considerations.” Id. at 
2716-17 (Kagan, J., dissenting). Nothing in the relevant language of the CWA relieves 
EPA from this obligation; in fact, EPA has recently asserted, and the U.S. Court of 
Appeals for the Ninth Circuit has agreed, that the language of the CWA allows EPA to 
consider compliance costs when approving state water quality standards or variances. 
Upper Missouri Waterkeeper v. US EPA, 15 F.4th 966, 972-74 (9th Cir. 2021) v (“EPA 
has reasonably construed § 1313(c)(2)(A) [CWA section 303(c)(2)(A)] as permitting it to 
consider compliance costs when approving water quality standards and variance 
requests”).  
 
The proposed rule fails to reasonably estimate and weigh the costs that would result from 
its implementation. Yet, Section 303(c)(4)(b) of the CWA – cited as authority for the 
proposed rule – requires the EPA Administrator to determine whether regulation “is 
necessary.” 88 FR at 29500 (emphasis added); 33 U.S.C. § 1313(c)(4)(B). This 
capacious term, “necessary,” requires the Administrator to reasonably consider all 
relevant factors, including cost, and the Agency must do so to avoid being arbitrary.  
 
EPA’s economic analysis of the proposed rule only estimates costs ranging from $15.51 
million to $36.45 million over a 20-year period, plus one-time costs ranging from $1.23 
million to $6.1 million. 88 FR at 29519; Economic Analysis, at p. 7, Exhibit ES-1. EPA 
asserts that the rule “is likely to yield significant benefits” but claims “estimating the 
dollar value of these improvements may not be feasible.” 88 FR at 29519. Nonetheless, 
EPA then asserts that “EPA anticipates, however, that the aforementioned benefits will 
ultimately outweigh the potential estimated costs associated with promulgation of this 
rule.…” Id.   
 
The proposed regulatory approach, which could result in unattainable water quality standards, is 
likely to impose far greater costs than estimated by EPA and create extreme regulatory 
uncertainty. If the standards are unattainable, the only recourse for the regulated facilities would 

 Coalition Comments 
 
17 
 
be an exception, such as a variance. However, variances are uncertain to be granted, subject to 
legal challenge, and are time-limited. Thus, even if a variance were granted, there is no guarantee 
that it would endure or be renewed into the future.   
 
In addition, the proposed rule would impose costs on regulated entities and non-point 
sources. While non-point sources are not typically subject to NPDES permitting, states 
and TAS tribes can and do regulate non-point sources through water quality standards, as 
mandated by CWA section 208(b)(2). 33 U.S.C. § 1288(b)(2). EPA must fully account 
for the costs on non-point sources in the rule. In sum, EPA must make a reasonable 
determination, in light of the full costs of the proposal, whether regulation is necessary to 
meet the requirements of the CWA.  
 
We stand ready to work with you to engage all stakeholders and urge you to withdraw the rule in 
the meantime. 
 
Sincerely, 
 
American Exploration & Mining Association 
American Farm Bureau Federation 
American Forest & Paper Association 
The Fertilizer Institute 
National Mining Association 
U.S. Chamber of Commerce

Dear Mr. Ray, 
Montana Department of Environmental Quality (DEQ) appreciates the opportunity to comment on the 
proposed rule, “Proposed Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations.” 
Montana DEQ is committed to continue collaboratively engaging with EPA under our cooperative 
federalism approach to administering Clean Water Act programs. DEQ is also committed to coordinating 
with our tribal and state partners to achieve shared goals of water quality protection. 
Montana DEQ supports the adoption of water quality standards that protect beneficial uses of water 
bodies and are based on sound scientific rationale. Montana DEQ also supports opportunities for public 
input to be provided as standards are adopted and implemented across Clean Water Act programs. 
Several Montana tribes have obtained TAS and have adopted water quality standards that have been 
approved by EPA; others are pursuing these actions. Montana DEQ applauds these efforts and 
appreciates EPA’s acknowledgement of these achievements in the proposed rule. 
This proposed rule will impact several Montana DEQ programs, including Montana Pollutant Discharge 
Elimination System (MPDES) permitting, beneficial use support and impairment assessment, Total 
Maximum Daily Load development, and our 401 and 404 certification programs. With this letter, 
Montana DEQ submits our comments on the proposed rule, “Proposed Federal Baseline Water Quality 
Standards for Indian Reservations.”
Applicability
States must be able to distinguish where the proposed federal baseline water quality standards apply 
and where state water quality standards apply. States must also accurately identify the spatial
boundaries of lands to which this rule applies to evaluate water body assessment unit boundaries and 
evaluate potential for downstream impacts of pollutant discharges.  EPA has noted difficulty in acquiring 
accurate spatial information depicting boundaries of certain categories of tribal lands. 
Montana DEQ requests that EPA maintain accurate maps that clearly distinguish the spatial boundaries 
of the formal and informal Indian reservation lands (including applicable trust lands) covered by this 
rule, as well as those lands that are considered exceptions including off-reservation allotments or 
dependent Indian communities and make them readily available to states and tribes. 

Page 2 of 3
NarraAve TranslaAon Procedures 
The proposed rule includes narrative criteria and binding translation procedures which EPA would use to 
determine case-specific numeric values protective of applicable designated uses for use in CWA program 
implementation. States must know the numeric values that are applicable to downstream waters to 
ensure adequate downstream protections when issuing permits. States also take upstream water quality
conditions and downstream protections into consideration when performing waterbody assessments, 
reasonable potential analysis, TMDL development, and other CWA program actions. 
Montana DEQ recommends EPA provide clarity and guidance related to the following technical aspects 
of the proposed rule’s narrative translation procedures:

Expectations for the type, quantity and quality of regional and location-specific information
which may be incorporated when selecting case-specific numeric values using the narrative 
translation procedures, including natural (nonanthropogenic) conditions.  

How EPA will ensure the most appropriate narrative translation procedure option is selected for 
a specific pollutant or circumstance. 

What timing and rationale is considered acceptable to retranslate the narrative for a pollutant 
to which the translator was already applied, particularly if the retranslation may result in a 
numeric value that differs from a previous translation outcome. 

How EPA will or will not ensure the same numeric values produced via narrative translation is 
implemented across all Clean Water Act programs. 

How differences, if they exist, between federal baseline water quality standards and adjacent 
state water quality standards will be reconciled when issuing permits or other CWA program 
actions. 
Montana DEQ recommends EPA provide clarity and guidance related to the following logistical aspects 
of the proposed rule’s narrative translation procedures:

How and when states may request that EPA perform a narrative standard translation for 
downstream waters. 

Who within EPA will perform the narrative translation to case-specific numeric values. 

Who will serve as EPA’s point of contact for coordination with states pertaining to narrative 
standard translation and implementation.  

How EPA will track translated numeric values and make them readily available to states, tribes, 
and the public. 
Public ParAcipaAon and InformaAon Sharing
Public participation is foundational to water quality standards development, adoption, and evaluation 
through time. 

Montana DEQ urges EPA to implement all reasonable public participation opportunities when 
implementing the proposed baseline WQS, including public notice and triennial review. 

Montana DEQ urges EPA to create a means of signing up to receive notification of significant 
actions related to this rule, including narrative translation, for states, tribes, and any interested 
persons.
Montana DEQ recommends that EPA develop a consolidated online platform for sharing information 
pertaining to this proposed rule, including notices of narrative translation, the numeric values that result 
from narrative translation procedures, comment opportunities, and other relevant information. 

Page 3 of 3
Best Available Science and DocumentaAon
Water quality standards must be based on based on sound scientific rationale and must protect 
designated uses of water bodies. Rigorous documentation and certainty in how the criteria were 
developed is foundational to water quality standards approval. 

Montana DEQ urges EPA to document the scientific rationale used when implementing the 
narrative standard and translation procedures, including documenting the rationale for the 
selected translation procedure option and the scientific basis of the numeric value produced.
EPA Regional Capacity and Impacts to State Clean Water Act Programs
Montana DEQ urges EPA to ensure that their regional offices receive adequate additional resources to 
enhance their capacity to perform additional workload related to this proposed rule. 

Montana DEQ urges EPA to consider additional resource demands that result from this 
proposed rule including but not limited to performing narrative translations, coordinating with 
states and tribes, issuing permits, performing and reporting on impairment determinations, and 
managing and sharing information related to the baseline water quality standards for Indian 
country. 
Montana DEQ urges EPA to communicate to states to what degree the practical implementation of this 
proposed rule may affect EPA regional offices’ capacity and resulting effects to states’ state Clean Water 
Act Programs, including: 

Review and approval of state-issued permits. 

Review and approval of Total Maximum Daily Load documents. 

Review and approval of 303d/305b water quality reports. 
Conclusion
Thank you again for the opportunity to provide comments on this proposed rule. We look forward to 
ongoing coordination with EPA, states, and tribes on water quality standards.

Standards for Indian Reservations 
 
Dear Administrator Regan: 
 
 
The undersigned States appreciate the opportunity to comment on the 
Environmental Protection Agency (“EPA”)’s proposal, Federal Baseline Water Quality 
Standards for Indian Reservations, 88 Fed. Reg. 29496 (May 5, 2023) (“Proposed 
Rule”), which would establish nationally applicable “baseline” federal water quality 
standards (“WQS”) under the Clean Water Act (“CWA”) for navigable waters on most 
“reservations.” We urge EPA to withdraw the Proposed Rule and to apply State-
promulgated water quality standards over the waters covered by this rule, as Congress 
intended.   
 
BACKGROUND 
 
In 1987, Congress amended the Clean Water Act to allow Tribes to be treated like 
States for some provisions of the CWA upon meeting certain requirements.1 Tribes with 
treatment-as-States (“TAS”) status have authority to establish WQS, administer permits, 
 
1  
The EPA Administrator may treat a Tribe as a State “only if” the following 
conditions are met: “(1) the Indian [T]ribe has a governing body carrying out substantial 
governmental duties and powers; (2) the functions to be exercised by the Indian [T]ribe 
pertain to the management and protection of water resources which are held by an Indian 
[T]ribe, held by the United States in trust for Indians, held by a member of an Indian tribe 
if such property interest is subject to a trust restriction on alienation, or otherwise within 
the borders of an Indian reservation; and (3) the Indian [T]ribe is reasonably expected to 
be capable, in the Administrator’s judgment, of carrying out the functions to be exercised 
in a manner consistent with the terms and purposes of this chapter and of all applicable 
regulations.” 33 U.S.C. § 1377(e).   

Administrator Michael Regan 
 
August 3, 2023 
Re: Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations 
Page 2 of 20 
 
and manage nonpoint source pollutants.2 Tribes that have not attained TAS status have no 
such authority. EPA’s role in the world of WQS is twofold: (1) to serve as a “backstop,”3 
stepping in only when a State or TAS Tribe is failing to meet the requirements of the 
Clean Water Act;4 and (2) to “support”5 States and TAS Tribes by providing technical 
services like § 304(a) national recommended water quality criteria for use by States and 
TAS Tribes when setting WQS.6  
 
The Proposed Rule would establish the following federal water quality standards 
for all navigable waters in non-TAS Tribal reservations,7 nationwide: 
 
 “Designated uses consistent with the CWA protection and 
restoration goals for aquatic life and users of surface water” 
including “a designated use that protects cultural and traditional 
uses”; 
 “water quality criteria to protect those uses” in narrative form, to 
be translated later by EPA using one of five binding “options”;

 “an antidegradation policy with associated implementation

maximum daily loads (“TMDLs”) and requirements for 
recognizing impaired waters and revising this status; and  
 “general WQS polic[i]es such as a mixing zone policy and

The Proposed Rule’s water quality criteria are in narrative form. EPA would not translate 
this narrative criteria into numeric criteria until implementation, when it would, on a site-
specific basis, select from 5 translation “options” to use to promulgate numeric criteria. 
The resulting numeric criteria would inform NDPES permits, EPA-issued § 401 
certifications, and § 404(c) vetoes over upstream projects.9  
 
The Proposed Rule rests on an assertion of authority under CWA § 303(c)(4)(B),10 
which requires EPA to first make a determination that a new or revised standard is 
necessary to meet the requirements of the CWA (a “necessity determination”11).   
 
EPA’s necessity determination is factual.  In the Proposed Rule, EPA discusses a 
gap in CWA protections, explaining that “[w]hile more than 300 Tribes within Indian 
reservations are eligible to apply for TAS, only 84 Tribes have applied and been 
approved to administer a WQS program”12 and that “[t]he lack of CWA-effective WQS 
for most Indian reservations means that those waters do not have the human health and 
environmental objectives in place that form the basis for CWA protections.”13  EPA 
estimates, accordingly, that roughly “76,000 miles of rivers and streams” and “1.9 million 
acres of lakes, reservoirs, and other open surface waters” are presently unprotected by the 
CWA.14  EPA notes the unprecedented speed at which Tribes are currently attaining TAS 
status (27, or approximately 33% of TAS Tribes, attained TAS status in the last 6 years), 
and the fact that “Tribal interest in obtaining TAS and adopting their own WQS has 
increased in recent years.”15  Yet, then EPA states that “acquiring TAS authorities and 
adopting WQS is a time and resource-intensive process,” and that “[a]t the current pace, 
it could take more than a decade for CWA-effective WQS to be put in place for all Indian

reservations”16 which is why, EPA concludes, the Proposed Rule is necessary.  In other 
words, EPA believes that: (1) there is a gap in CWA protections over waters on 
reservations of non-TAS Tribes; (2) this gap exists because Tribes are not attaining TAS 
Status fast enough; and (3) it is EPA’s prerogative to promulgate comprehensive, 
nationwide water quality criteria to fill this gap.   
 
DISCUSSION 
 
EPA is charged with protecting human health and the environment under the 
authority conferred and circumscribed by Congress. In the WQS context, Congress has 
required EPA to play a supportive role to States as they protect the health of their 
environment while juggling multiple other policy considerations, like protecting the 
health of their economy and providing for the overall well-being of their people. In its 
1987 amendments, Congress carved out a similar role for EPA vis-a-vis Tribes who have 
met Congress’s TAS requirements. The Proposed Rule upsets this considered division of 
responsibility. For reasons detailed below, EPA lacks authority to promulgate this rule 
and it is arbitrary and capricious.  
 
1. 
EPA lacks authority to promulgate this rule. 
 
The Proposed Rule relies on the authority conferred by CWA § 303(c)(4). This 
provision requires that the EPA Administrator “promptly prepare and publish proposed 
regulations setting forth a revised or new water quality standard for the navigable waters 
involved” in two situations: 
 
(A) 
if a revised or new water quality standard submitted by such 
State under paragraph (3) of this subsection for such waters is 
determined by the Administrator not to be consistent with the 
applicable requirements of this chapter; or  
(B) 
in any case where the Administrator determines that a revised 
or new standard is necessary to meet the requirements of this 
chapter. 
 
 
16  
Id. 

Administrator Michael Regan 
 
August 3, 2023 
Re: Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations 
Page 5 of 20 
 
The Proposed Rule invokes only subsection (B).17 To lawfully invoke this power, EPA 
must make a “necessity determination.”18 Assuming a valid necessity determination, EPA 
is “subject to the same policies, procedures, analyses, and public participation 
requirements established for States in these regulations” when promulgating WQS.19 
 
 
As detailed below, the Proposed Rule: (a) disregards Congress’s intent that State 
WQS apply in Tribal navigable waters until Tribes attain TAS status and promulgate 
Tribe-specific WQS; (b) exceeds Congress’s limited grant of power to EPA under 
§ 303(c)(4)(B); and (c) rests on a demonstrably false necessity determination. 
 
a. 
EPA disregards Congress’s intent that State WQS apply in Tribal 
navigable waters until Tribes attain TAS status and promulgate Tribe-
specific WQS.  
 
States have spent more than 50 years establishing science-based water quality 
standards that recognize each State’s unique topography, hydrogeology, geology, climate, 
and the resulting rivers, streams, and lakes that make up the State’s surface waters. Not 
only is relying on State WQS to protect reservation waters sensible, adherent to 
cooperative federalism precepts, and consistent with EPA past policy—it is required by 
the CWA. Congress has plenary authority over Indian affairs,20 which includes Tribal 
navigable waters regulatable under the CWA. Determining which WQS Congress 
intended to apply to non-TAS reservation waters is a straightforward matter of statutory 
construction.   
 
 
17  
EPA points to a previous EPA’s Administrator’s necessity determination. 88 Fed. 
Reg. at 28955. Since this determination never made it past the draft stage before being 
withdrawn, EPA is for all practical purposes simply re-adopting this previous statement 
as its own. EPA cannot evade accountability by pointing to something that was never 
finalized.

Gulf Restoration Network, 783 F.3d at 230.

Haaland v. Brackeen, 143 S. Ct. 1609, 1629 (June 15, 2023) (“Congress’s power 
to legislate with respect to Indians is well established and broad.”).

Re: Federal Baseline Water Quality Standards for Indian Reservations 
Page 6 of 20 
 
The CWA’s comprehensive nationwide environmental regulatory structure does 
not omit Tribal waters from the scope of State-promulgated WQS protections.21 Under 
the CWA, States have the primary right, responsibility, and authority to plan for the 
development and use of water resources and to create, review, and revise WQS for “all 
intrastate waters.”22 The CWA requirement that States promulgate WQS for all intrastate 
waters, by its plain language, is necessarily inclusive of all Tribal reservation waters 
within State boundaries.23   
 
Supreme Court precedent supports this reading. In Federal Power Commission v. 
Tuscarora Indian Nation, the Supreme Court recognized that “a general statute in terms 
applying to all persons includes Indians and their property interests.” 24 The analogous 
context of the Safe Drinking Water Act, which establishes “national policy with respect 
to clean water,” was broadly held to apply to Tribal lands consistent with Tuscarora rule, 
prior to Congress’s amendment explicitly stating so.25 EPA’s own WQS regulations and

(EPA acknowledgment that CWA is designed to ensure that “all waters” are sufficiently 
clean); id. at 60851 (EPA’s initial efforts under the 1972 CWA aimed to expand coverage 
to “all interstate and intrastate surface waters”).

by specific benchmarked deadlines in the early 1970s. 33 U.S.C. § 1313(a)–(b). See also 
PUD No. 1, 511 U.S. at 704 (stating that the CWA is a “comprehensive water quality 
statute” that establishes “distinct roles for the Federal and State Governments” and 
“requires each State, subject to federal approval, to institute comprehensive water quality 
standards establishing water quality goals for all intrastate waters”); Coeur Alaska, Inc. v. 
Southeast Alaska Conservation Council, 557 U.S. 261, 299 n.2 (2009).

Page 7 of 20 
 
historical guidance on WQS, too, emphasize the role of States in comprehensively 
promulgating WQS for all intrastate waters.26   
 
Examining the timing of CWA amendments confirms this understanding. The 
CWA required States to comprehensively promulgate WQS for intrastate waters by 
specific benchmarked deadlines in the early 1970s. The CWA’s Tribal TAS provisions 
were added later, in 1987, and only thereafter permitted eligible Tribes, if approved for 
TAS by EPA, to be treated as if they were States for WQS purposes on a prospective 
basis.27 Congress’s intent was plainly for State WQS to protect all navigable waters 
unless and until Tribes attain TAS status and promulgate Tribe-specific WQS. Congress 
did not mistakenly legislate a “gap” in coverage into the CWA, consisting of non-TAS 
waters. 
 
 
EPA’s § 303(c)(4)(B) power is narrow and limited, and cannot support such a 
broad and sweeping rule. Invocation of this power requires a valid necessity 
determination, which EPA has not made. Congress’s intent is that State WQS apply until 
TAS Tribes promulgate Tribe-specific WQS, which EPA is not free to disregard. 
 
b. 
The Proposed Rule exceeds EPA’s power under § 303(c)(4)(B). 
 
In 1989, EPA undertook a rulemaking to establish the criteria and procedures by 
which a Tribe can qualify for TAS status.  Speaking about federal promulgation of water 
quality standards, EPA stated in this rulemaking that:
